Many-Body Superconductivity in Topological Flat Bands
Jonah Herzog-Arbeitman 1, Aaron Chew 1, Kukka-Emilia Huhtinen 2, Päivi Törmä 2, B. Andrei Bernevig 1,3,4

Abstract
In a flat band superconductor, bosonic excitations can disperse while unpaired electrons are immobile. To study this strongly interacting system, we construct a family of multi-band Hubbard models with exact eta-pairing ground states in all space groups. We analytically compute their many-body excitations and find that the Cooper pair bound states and density excitations obey an effective single-particle Hamiltonian written in terms of the non-interacting wavefunctions. These bound states possess a unique zero-energy excitation whose quadratic dispersion is determined by the minimal quantum metric. The rest of the bound state spectrum is classified by topological quantum chemistry, which we use to identify Cooper pairs with Weyl nodes, higher angular momentum pairing, and fragile topology. We also add electron kinetic energy as a perturbation to show that the strongest pairing occurs at half filling and not at the highest density of states. This is similar in spirit to the superconductivity observed in twisted bilayer graphene.



Attractive interactions among electrons can lead to a pairing instability at the Fermi surface and create a superconducting ground state at low temperatures. Both the weakly and strongly interacting limits of these systems can be described by attractive Hubbard models. Such models, however, are rarely exactly solvable. Recent work
Törmä et al. 2022
;
Kopnin et al. 2011
;
Julku et al. 2016
;
Törmä et al. 2018
;
Tovmasyan et al. 2018
;
Julku et al. 2020
;
Tian et al. 2021
;
Peri et al. 2021
;
Hu et al. 2019
;
Huhtinen and Törmä 2021
;
Wang et al. 2021a
;
Iskin 2021
;
Žitko and Pavešić 2022
;
Kitamura et al. 2022
has generated great interest in flat band superconductivity where electrons are completely immobile without interactions. A mean-field calculation in the multiband Hubbard model shows
Peotta and Törmä 2015
that the superfluid weight can be non-zero due to quantum geometry
Resta 2011
, indicating mobile Cooper pairs despite the infinite effective mass of the unpaired electrons. Lower bounds on the superfluid weight have been obtained from topology
Peotta and Törmä 2015
;
Xie et al. 2020
;
Tovmasyan et al. 2016
and symmetry
Herzog-Arbeitman et al. 2022
. While numerical beyond-mean-field work exists
Julku et al. 2016
;
Peri et al. 2021
;
Herzog-Arbeitman et al. 2022
;
Chan et al. 2022
;
Chan et al. 2022
;
Hofmann et al. 2022
;
Hofmann et al. 2020
;
Orso and Singh 2022
and confirms the mean-field picture, the few exact approaches on the attractive Hubbard model governing flat band superconductivity have been limited to ground state properties
Tovmasyan et al. 2016
or one dimension
Tovmasyan et al. 2018
. In this Letter, we show that the
many-body
problem is analytically tractable in any dimension subject to a simple requirement called the uniform pairing condition
Peotta and Törmä 2015
;
Tovmasyan et al. 2016
. Our exact results characterize not only the superconducting ground state, but also its excitations which control the low-temperature behavior.

We consider tight-binding models with electron operators
c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}
with spin
\sigma=\uparrow,\downarrow
, orbital
\alpha=1,\dots,N_{orb}
, and momentum
\mathbf{k}
in the Brillouin zone (BZ). Throughout, we assume
S_{z}
spin conservation, e.g. no spin-orbit coupling. We consider kinetic terms with flat bands at zero energy labeled by
m=1,\dots,N_{f}
separated by a large gap to the other bands (see Fig.
1
b for example). We assume the gap is much larger than the interaction strength
|U|
. Under this approximation, the kinetic term is completely characterized by the single-particle eigenvectors of the flat bands which form the
N_{orb}\times N_{f}
matrix
[U_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})]_{\alpha m}
. By orthonormality,
U^{\dagger}_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})U_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})=\mathbb{1}_{N_{f}}
, and
P^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})=[U_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})U^{\dagger}_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})]_{\alpha\beta}
is a rank
N_{f}
projector, or
\mathbf{k}
-space correlation function
Bernevig and Hughes 2013
. The gap to the other bands can be sent adiabatically to infinity so all operators can be projected into the flat bands spanned by

\displaystyle\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\beta,\sigma}P^{\sigma}_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k}),\ \{\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\alpha,\sigma},\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\beta,\sigma}\}=\delta_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}P^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})

(1)

which form an overcomplete basis due to the projection (App.
A
). The canonical flat band operators
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}=\sum_{\alpha}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}U_{\alpha,m}^{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})
alternatively form a complete basis of the projected Hilbert space. For brevity, we define
P(\mathbf{k})=P^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{k})
. We assume
S_{z}
conservation and spinful time-reversal symmetry
\mathcal{T}
which yields
P^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{k})=P(-\mathbf{k})^{*}
.

Hamiltonian.
The
N_{f}
flat bands have zero kinetic energy and the remaining bands are projected out, so the kinetic term is zero and only interactions remain. We study an attractive Hubbard model proposed in Ref.
Tovmasyan et al. 2016
,

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\frac{|U|}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha}(\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}-\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow})^{2},\ \ \bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}=\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}

(2)

where
\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}
is the Fourier transform of
\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}
. If we impose the uniform pairing condition (UPC)
Peotta and Törmä 2015
;
Tovmasyan et al. 2016

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{N_{f}}{N_{orb}}\equiv\epsilon\leq 1

(3)

where the
\alpha
-independent constant
\epsilon
is fixed by
\text{Tr}\ P(\mathbf{k})=N_{f}
, and
\mathcal{N}
is the number of unit cells, then we find

\displaystyle H=\frac{\epsilon|U|}{2}\bar{N}-\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}

(4)

which is the usual Hubbard model up to a chemical potential (note that
\bar{N}=\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\sigma}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}
is the projected number operator). The UPC thus allows to relate the flat band Hubbard Hamiltonian to the positive-definite Hamiltonian Eq. (
2
), which is
crucial to analytically solving the ground states and excitations.

Intuitively, if the orbitals are related by symmetry, the UPC should hold
Tovmasyan et al. 2016
. Indeed, we prove that Eq. (
3
) is enforced by symmetry. Our proof (App.
A.2
) uses Schur’s lemma and some basic facts from topological quantum chemistry
Bradlyn et al. 2017
;
Cano and Bradlyn 2020
;
Bacry et al. 1988
;
Aroyo et al. 2006a
;
Aroyo et al. 2006b
. We show that if the orbitals of the model can all be related by a symmetry operator (technically, if they form an irrep of a Wyckoff position), then the UPC holds. Thus the class of flat bands with the UPC is very large, and encompasses existing examples
Peri et al. 2021
;
Herzog-Arbeitman et al. 2022
;
Bergman et al. 2008
;
Rhim and Yang 2019
;
Song et al. 2020a
;
Huhtinen and Törmä 2021
;
Törmä et al. 2018
;
Tovmasyan et al. 2018
.

There is a generalization
Tovmasyan et al. 2016
of Eq. (
3
): if the flat band wavefunction
[U_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})]_{\alpha m}
vanishes on some orbitals
\tilde{\alpha}
, then
\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\tilde{\alpha},\sigma}=0
and the UPC is only required on
\alpha\neq\tilde{\alpha}
. Calugaru et al.
Cǎlugǎru et al. 2021
construct models with
N_{f}=N_{L}-N_{\tilde{L}}
perfectly flat bands on bipartite lattices consisting of two sublattices
L,\tilde{L}
with
[U_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})]_{\tilde{\alpha}m}=0
where
N_{L}\,(N_{\tilde{L}})
is the number of orbitals in
L\,(\tilde{L})
per unit cell. Choosing the
L
lattice to have the UPC, their method constructs exactly flat bands with the UPC in all space groups. In this case, Eq. (
3
) holds with
\epsilon=N_{f}/N_{L}
. In this manner, we construct a 10 band model (see Fig.
1
) with the momentum space irreps of twisted bilayer graphene (TBG)
Bistritzer and MacDonald 2011
;
Song et al. 2019
;
Po et al. 2018
and the UPC (see Fig.
1
), but with Wilson loop winding
Ahn et al. 2019
;
Bouhon et al. 2018
;
Yu et al. 2022
2 (instead of 1 as in TBG).

Figure 1:
Ten band model inspired by TBG. (
a
) A bipartite lattice of
s
(
p
) orbitals at the 6j (2b) Wyckoff positions reproduces the momentum space irreps
Song et al. 2019
;
Ahn et al. 2019
;
Po et al. 2018
of the Bistritzer-MacDonald (BM) model
Bistritzer and MacDonald 2011
with uniform pairing. (
b
) Band structure of the 10-band model (red) and BM model (black) with Wilson loop inset. (
c
) Cooper pair spectrum with the minimal quantum metric approximation in blue.
p
-wave pairing Leggett modes appear above the zero-energy
s
-wave mode (App.
F
). (
d
) Superfluid weight
D_{s}
from mean-field theory in units where
e,\hbar,k_{B}=1
. The BKT temperature
T_{c}=\frac{\pi}{8}D_{s}(T_{c})
is estimated at
T_{c}\approx 0.06|U|
.

Superconducting Ground States.
Originally,
H
in Eq. (
2
) was proposed to exhibit an exact BCS-type groundstate despite the ill-defined Fermi surface of the flat band
Tovmasyan et al. 2016
. In this work, we instead focus on the more physical ground states at
fixed
particle number. We define the local spin operator
\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}=\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}-\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}
appearing in Eq. (
2
). Because of the projection,
\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}
is extended on the scale of the correlation length, which cannot be adiabatically sent to zero (the atomic limit) if the bands are topological or obstructed
Herzog-Arbeitman et al. 2022
. This is crucial for superconductivity in transport since trivial flat bands in the atomic limit would make
H=\frac{|U|}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}(\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha})^{2}
a sum of commuting local terms with trivial dynamics and macroscopic degeneracies. Away from the trivial limit,
H
retains a single nontrivial symmetry operator
\eta

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\eta^{\dagger},\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}]=0,\ \eta^{\dagger}=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},\alpha,\downarrow}=\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}\ .

(5)

This result relies on the UPC, global
S_{z}
conservation, and
\mathcal{T}
(App.
B
). Here
\eta^{\dagger}
is simply the creation operator of a zero-momentum
s
-wave Cooper pair, and it forms an
su(2)
algebra,
[\bar{N},\eta^{\dagger}]=2\eta^{\dagger}
, which extends
U(1)
charge conservation
Tovmasyan et al. 2016
. We define the states
\ket{n}\propto\eta^{{\dagger}n}\ket{0}
which have fixed particle number
\bar{N}\ket{n}=2n\ket{n}
for
n=0,\dots,\mathcal{N}N_{f}
. Yang first introduced similar eta-pairing states
Yang 1989
on the square lattice, which is also bipartite. However, his operators carried momentum
\pi
and were not ground states. In contrast,
\ket{n}
must be ground states because
H
is positive semi-definite, and
H\ket{n}\propto\eta^{{\dagger}n}H\ket{0}=0
.

Because
\ket{n}
differs from the BCS-BEC
Chen et al. 2005
;
Randeria and Taylor 2013
states
e^{z\eta^{\dagger}}\ket{0}
(which are also exact ground states
Tovmasyan et al. 2016
), it may not be apparent that they describe a bosonic condensate. To establish this, we show that
\ket{n}
has off-diagonal long-range order in the two-particle density matrix
Yang 1962
; this is intuitive since the
\eta
pairs are superpositions of pairs on
all
sites. In the states
\ket{n}
, a generalized Wick’s theorem
Peotta 2022
;
Molinari 2017
;
Fetter and Walecka 1971
holds for the correlators of the Wannier state operators
w^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}m\sigma}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha}e^{i\mathbf{R}\cdot\mathbf{k}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}
obeying
\{w_{\mathbf{R},m,\sigma},w^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},m^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\}=\delta_{\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}\delta_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}
. We find (App.
C
)

\displaystyle\lim_{|\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}|\to\infty}\braket{n|w^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}m^{\prime}\downarrow}w^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}m^{\prime}\uparrow}w_{\mathbf{R}m\uparrow}w_{\mathbf{R}m\downarrow}|n}=\nu(1-\nu)

(6)

in the thermodynamic limit where
\nu=n/{N_{f}\mathcal{N}}
is the filling. Clearly the strongest condensate appears at half-filling of the flat bands. Similar dependence on the filling appears in mean-field results on the multi-band Hubbard model
Peotta and Törmä 2015
which also indicate half-filling is favorable for the superconductor. We remark that the Wannier operators have a gauge freedom corresponding to the redefinition of eigenvectors
U_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})\to U_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})
where
\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})\in U(N_{f})

Marzari et al. 2012
;
Brouder et al. 2007
;
Bernevig et al. 2006
;
Monaco et al. 2018
;
Monaco et al. 2016
;
Winkler et al. 2016
;
Soluyanov and Vanderbilt 2011
;
Song et al. 2020b
;
Song et al. 2020c
. Nevertheless, Eq. (
6
) shows that the resulting correlator is gauge-invariant. This condensate ground state is a universal result in all UPC lattices.

Electron Excitations.
The presence of an enlarged symmetry algebra and exact ground states suggests that more features of
H
may be accessible. We now show that the electron (and hole) excitations above the
\ket{n}
ground states are exactly solvable. Our procedure
Bernevig et al. 2021
;
Kang et al. 2021
;
Bernevig et al. 2021
;
Herzog-Arbeitman et al. 2022
;
Kang and Vafek 2019
is to add a single electron
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}
to the ground state and calculate

\displaystyle\hbox{}H\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}\ket{n}=[H,\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}]\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m^{\prime}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},m^{\prime},\sigma}\ket{n}[R^{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})]_{m^{\prime}m}

(7)

since
H\ket{n}=0
. Diagonalizing
R^{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})
, one obtains the charge
+1
(electron) excitation spectrum and the eigenstates. Using
H=\frac{|U|}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{q}\alpha}\bar{S}^{z}_{-\mathbf{q},\alpha}\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{q},\alpha}
where
\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{q},\alpha}
is the Fourier transform of
\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}
and
\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{q},\alpha}\ket{n}=0
, we find

\displaystyle\hbox{}[H,\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}]\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{|U|}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{q}\alpha}[\bar{S}^{z}_{-\mathbf{q},\alpha},[\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{q},\alpha},\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}]]\ket{n}

(8)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!=\frac{|U|}{2\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}\alpha,m^{\prime}l}M^{m^{\prime}l}_{\sigma,\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},-\mathbf{q})M^{lm}_{\sigma,\alpha}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},m^{\prime},\sigma}\ket{n}

defining
M^{mn}_{\alpha,\sigma}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})=U^{\sigma*}_{\alpha m}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})U^{\sigma}_{\alpha n}(\mathbf{k})
as the orbital-resolved form factors (App.
D.1
). Matching Eq. (
8
) to Eq. (
7
) and simplifying the form factors yields

\displaystyle\hbox{}[R^{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})]_{m^{\prime}m}

\displaystyle=\frac{|U|}{2\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}\alpha}U^{\sigma*}_{\alpha m^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})P^{\sigma}_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q})U^{\sigma}_{\alpha m}(\mathbf{k})

(9)

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon|U|}{2}\sum_{\alpha}U^{\sigma*}_{\alpha m^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})U^{\sigma}_{\alpha m}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{\epsilon|U|}{2}\delta_{m^{\prime}m}

where the UPC, Eq. (
3
), was crucial to compute the
\mathbf{q}
sum. Hence, the electron excitations are independent of
\nu
,
N_{f}
-degenerate per spin, and exactly flat meaning that the single-electron excitations are immobile. Indeed, a mean-field treatment assuming a
\mathbf{k}
-independent pairing gap reproduces the
\epsilon|U|/2

s
-wave gap
Huhtinen et al. 2022
. The temperature scale
\epsilon|U|
describes the onset of pairing, but is expected to be somewhat above the superconducting critical temperature with a pseudogap intervening
Wang et al. 2020
;
Zhang et al. 2021a
;
Hashimoto et al. 2014
;
Tovmasyan et al. 2018
.

The electronic excitations characterize the stability of the superconducting phase, as in BCS theory. When dispersion is added to the flat bands, as in realistic systems, we determine the filling where superconductivity is strongest through the Richardson criterion
Richardson and Sherman 1964
;
Richardson 1977
;
Bernevig et al. 2021

E_{\Delta}(N+2)=E(N+2)-2E(N+1)+E(N)
where
E(N)
is the ground state energy at particle number
N
. Here
E_{\Delta}
is an estimate of the Cooper pair binding energy:
E_{\Delta}<0
indicates the favorability of pair formation (at
T=0
). We can compute
E_{\Delta}(N)
perturbatively when dispersion
\tilde{E}_{n}(\mathbf{k})
is added to the flat bands. For convenience, we assume
\sum_{\mathbf{k},n}\tilde{E}_{n}(\mathbf{k})=0
in which case (App.
D.2
)

\displaystyle-E_{\Delta}(\nu)=\epsilon|U|-|1-2\nu|\tilde{E}_{\nu}

(10)

where
\tilde{E}_{\nu}>0
is
\max_{\mathbf{k},n}\tilde{E}_{n}(\mathbf{k})
(
|\min_{\mathbf{k},n}\tilde{E}_{n}(\mathbf{k})|)
for
\nu\geq 1/2
(
\nu<1/2
). Hence the strongest Cooper pair binding occurs at
\nu=\frac{1}{2}
, where the condensate is also strongest (see Eq. (
6
)) in agreement with multi-band mean-field results
Peotta and Törmä 2015
, but in contrast to conventional BCS theory where pairing is maximized at the highest density of states. This echoes the phenomenology of twisted bilayer graphene where critical temperature is highest
not
at the van Hove singularities of the flat bands
Cao et al. 2018
;
Oh et al. 2021
;
Park et al. 2020
;
Stepanov et al. 2020
;
Xie et al. 2019
. Eq. (
10
) is not valid when spin-orbit coupling is added, but we provide a general formula in App.
D.2
.

Cooper Pair Excitations and Bound States.
We now show that the two-particle excitations, containing the Cooper pair and density excitation spectrum, are analytically solvable. We will also find a set of excitations (Leggett modes
Leggett 1966
) in other pairing channels. We find that these modes can carry higher angular momentum, e.g.
d
-wave symmetry, and fragile topology. Our calculation shows in microscopic detail the formation of bosons from the underlying fermion bands.

The two-body excitations are characterized by their global quantum numbers,
S_{z}
spin and charge. We start in the charge
+2
sector and compute the scattering matrix

\displaystyle[H,\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},n,\sigma^{\prime}}]\ket{n}

(11)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m^{\prime},\sigma}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\ket{n}[R^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p})]_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime},\mathbf{k}mn}

which is a function of the total momentum
\mathbf{p}
. The commutator can be computed since
\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\ket{n}=0
. The full scattering matrix can be written (App.
D.3
)

\displaystyle R^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\epsilon|U|\mathbb{1}+s^{z}_{\sigma}s^{z}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\frac{|U|}{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{U}_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p})\mathcal{U}_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{p})

(12)

where
s^{z}_{\uparrow/\downarrow}=\pm 1
and
[\mathcal{U}_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p})]_{\mathbf{k}mn,\alpha}=U^{\sigma*}_{m\alpha}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})U^{\sigma^{\prime}*}_{n\alpha}(-\mathbf{k})
is a
\mathcal{N}N_{f}^{2}\times N_{L}
matrix (
\mathcal{N}
is the number of
\mathbf{k}
points). If
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{U}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mathbf{p})
is symmetric under
\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m\leftrightarrow-\mathbf{k},n
whereas the fermion operators in Eq. (
11
) are anti-symmetric. Hence the anti-symmetrization of the fermions annihilates the
\mathcal{U}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mathbf{p})\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}_{\sigma\sigma}(\mathbf{p})
term, so all spin-aligned states have energy
\epsilon|U|
for all
\mathbf{p}
and are completely unpaired.

We now consider
\sigma=-\sigma^{\prime}
. Denote
R^{\uparrow\downarrow}(\mathbf{p})=\epsilon|U|\mathbb{1}-\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{p})\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{p})/\mathcal{N}
, dropping spin labels for brevity. First, note that
\text{rank }\mathcal{U}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\leq\text{rank }\mathcal{U}\leq N_{L}
and hence
\mathcal{U}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}
has a very large kernel of dimension at least
\mathcal{N}N_{f}^{2}-N_{L}
, corresponding to the particle-particle continuum. Its nonzero eigenvalues are identical to the eigenvalues of the
N_{L}\times N_{L}
matrix
h(\mathbf{p})=\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{p})\mathcal{U}(\mathbf{p})/\mathcal{N}
. This pairing Hamiltonian takes the universal form (App.
D.3
)

\displaystyle h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{p})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})

(13)

which can be interpreted as the effective single-particle Hamiltonian describing the hopping of a tightly bound Cooper pair (App.
E.2
). However, we emphasize that the Eq. (
13
) derives from an exact many-body calculation, and its eigenvalues are precisely the binding energies of the various pairing channels.

We denote the eigen-decompositon of Eq. (
13
) as
h(\mathbf{p})u_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})=\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})u_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})
for
\mu=0,\dots,N_{L}-1
. The many-body energies of
R^{\uparrow\downarrow}(\mathbf{p})
are
\epsilon|U|
for the
\mathcal{N}N_{f}^{2}-N_{L}
unpaired states and
E_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})=|U|(\epsilon-\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p}))
for the
N_{L}
bound states ( Fig.
1
c). Returning to Eq. (
11
), the pair excitations can be written as
\eta^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p},\mu}\ket{n}
where (App.
D.3
)

\displaystyle\eta^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p},\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha}\frac{u^{\alpha}_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}{\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},\alpha,\downarrow}

(14)

which is the many-body analogue of the BCS pairing, with
\mu
enumerating the pairing channels. We will show that
\eta^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p},\mu}
can carry nontrivial topology protected by
G
.

We remark that the charge-0 density excitations are also exactly solvable. We determine (App.
D.5
) that the spin
\pm 1
density excitations are gapped with energies above the
\epsilon|U|
pairing threshold. The two spin-0 density excitations, of the form
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},\sigma}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},\sigma}
, in fact have a spectrum
identical
to the spin-0 Cooper pairs. We will show momentarily that there is guaranteed to be a gapless Goldstone mode in this spectrum, similar to the Anderson-Bogoliubov phonon
Pitaevskii et al. 2003
;
Feynman 1954
;
Zwerger 2011
.
However our calculation shows this Goldstone mode is quadratic (App.
E.3
), whereas
the Anderson-Bogoliubov phonon typically has linear dispersion. The quadratic dispersion can be attributed to the breaking of the
\eta,\eta^{\dagger}
generators by fixing the particle number
Watanabe 2019
;
Zhang et al. 2021a
.

Boson Bands and Topology.
We have reduced the two-body scattering matrix to an effective band theory problem given by the Hamiltonian in Eq. (
13
). Notably, this result is generic: it is valid for all lattices with the UPC. We now prove a number of universal features of
h(\mathbf{p})
.

First,
\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\geq 0
. This can be proved from the Schur product theorem
Prasolov 1994
, which states that the Hadamard product
[A\circ B]_{\alpha\beta}=A_{\alpha\beta}B_{\alpha\beta}
of positive semi-definite matrices is positive semi-definite. Therefore
h(\mathbf{p})
is the sum of positive semi-definite terms, and is also positive semi-definite. Secondly,
\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\leq\epsilon
follows since Eq. (
2
) is the sum of squares and hence
E_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})=|U|(\epsilon-\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p}))>0
. Note that the largest eigenvalue of
h(\mathbf{p})
, i.e. the strongest pairing, corresponds to the lowest energy many-body state. We number the bands such that
\epsilon_{0}(\mathbf{p})\geq\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})
.

Because
\eta^{\dagger}
is a zero-energy,
s
-wave, two-body excitation with
E_{0}(\mathbf{0})=0
, it follows that
\epsilon_{0}(\mathbf{0})=\epsilon
(App.
E.2
). To study the small
\mathbf{p}
behavior around
\epsilon_{0}(\mathbf{0})=\epsilon
, we apply perturbation theory. The result is
E_{0}(\mathbf{p})=\frac{|U|}{N_{L}}g_{ij}p_{i}p_{j}+O(p^{4})
where
g_{ij}
is the integrated minimal quantum metric
Huhtinen et al. 2022
(App.
E.3
). This amounts to a many-body proof of the result first reported in mean-field
Peotta and Törmä 2015
and by a two-body calculation
Törmä et al. 2018
. Notably, the bipartite construction
Cǎlugǎru et al. 2021
produces many examples of fragile or obstructed single-particle bands, for which
g_{ij}
is lower bounded
Herzog-Arbeitman et al. 2022
;
Yu et al. 2022
.
Hence, the low-energy spectrum is universally governed by the quantum geometry of the flat bands.
The presence of tightly bound pairs with quadratic dispersion suggests these models are in the Bose-Einstein condensate regime of superconductivity
Randeria and Taylor 2013
;
Chen et al. 2005
;
Julku et al. 2021
;
Zwerger 2011
. Transport in the superconductor can be characterized by the
T=0
mean-field superfluid weight
D_{s}
, shown in Fig.
1
(d). For degenerate flat
bands, a mean-field calculation shows (App.
G
)

[D_{s}]_{ij}=8\frac{\epsilon|U|}{V_{c}}\nu(1-\nu)g_{ij},\quad g_{ij}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}g_{ij}(\mathbf{k})

(15)

where
V_{c}
is the volume of a unit cell and
g_{ij}(\mathbf{k})
is the minimal quantum metric of the degenerate bands
Xie et al. 2020
;
Huhtinen et al. 2022
;
Mera and Mitscherling 2022
;
Herzog-Arbeitman et al. 2022
. Eq. (
15
) predicts
D_{s}/|U|\approx 0.277
for our model, in good agreement with Fig.
1
d.

The eigenvalues of
h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})
correspond to the pairing channels, with
s
-wave pairing at low energies, but higher energy modes with nontrivial pairing symmetries appear (see Fig.
1
c) and contribute to the finite temperature behavior. Although the zero-energy
s
-wave mode is universal across all space groups
G
, the higher energy spectrum varies. Nevertheless, we can use topological quantum chemistry to determine all possible Cooper pair band connectivities
Bradlyn et al. 2018
;
Christensen et al. 2021
;
Elcoro et al. 2020a
;
Watanabe et al. 2018
;
Kruthoff et al. 2017
and topology for each
G
(App.
E
).

The pairing Hamiltonian
h(\mathbf{p})
in an effective tight-binding model which inherits
bosonic
single-particle symmetries from the underlying electron space group
G
. Eq. (
13
) shows
h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})=h^{*}_{\alpha\beta}(-\mathbf{p})
so
h(\mathbf{p})
has a spinless time-reversal symmetry. We can also show (App.
E.4
) that
h(\mathbf{p})
inherits the space group
G
but in the real representation
|D_{\alpha\beta}[g]|,g\in G
, where
D_{\alpha\beta}[g]
is the representation of the spin-
\uparrow
electrons
Alexandradinata et al. 2020
. The possible band structures with these orbitals are highly constrained by topological quantum chemistry since, by enforcing the UPC within our construction, the orbitals of
h(\mathbf{p})
form a single Wyckoff position
Cano and Bradlyn 2020
and are symmetry-related. If the orbitals are at low-symmetry (non-maximal Wyckoff) positions, then the bands of
h(\mathbf{p})
are composite and can be deformed into disconnected atomic representations
Cano et al. 2018a
. If the orbitals are located at high-symmetry (maximal Wyckoff) positions, the bands are either all connected through high-symmetry points of the BZ or decomposable into
topological
bands
Bradlyn et al. 2018
;
Cano et al. 2018b
. However, because
h(\mathbf{p})
has a spinless time-reversal symmetry, no stable topology is possible
Brouder et al. 2007
;
Fang et al. 2012
. Instead, a non-zero symmetry indicator
Bradlyn et al. 2017
;
Po 2020
;
Po et al. 2017
;
Kruthoff et al. 2017
identifies the presence of topologically protected Weyl nodes
Hughes et al. 2011
;
Elcoro et al. 2020b
. In 2D, we check exhaustively that the only groups with fragile topological Cooper pairs are
G=p6,p6mm
. In
p6mm
, placing
s
or
p
orbitals at the kagome (3c) position induces a unique decomposition: the low-energy branch is a topologically trivial
s
-wave Cooper pair, and there is a gap to two connected bands with fragile topology where the Cooper pair wavefunction interpolates between
d
-wave and
p
-wave character across the BZ (App.
D.4
). This decomposition is also possible in
p6
, but there is also a second decomposition with a nonzero symmetry indicator
\theta_{2}\in\mathds{Z}_{2}
. In this case, the Cooper pair bands are connected by Weyl nodes. An exhaustive search of the 3D Wyckoff positions with the UPC yields 22 space groups with decomposable bands (App.
E.5
). We find instances where the low-energy branch is fragile topological. All 22 are kagome/pyrochlore groups where many promising flat band candidates
Regnault et al. 2022
have been identified. Our classification identifies flat band superconductors which host topological Cooper pairs and higher angular momentum spin-singlet pairing due to the multi-orbital lattice.

Conclusions.
Although the BCS paradigm is successful in describing weakly-coupled superconductors, the numerous unresolved questions in high-
T_{c}
materials
Joynt and Taillefer 2002
;
Sigrist and Ueda 1991
;
Lee et al. 2006
and moiré heterostructures
Balents et al. 2020
;
Törmä et al. 2022
;
Zhang et al. 2021b
;
Park et al. 2021
;
Lake et al. 2022
;
Wang et al. 2021b
;
Lian et al. 2019
;
Zhang et al. 2021a
demonstrate a pressing need for a strong-coupling theory of superconductivity
Zhang et al. 2021a
;
Lapa and Levin 2020
;
Lapa 2020
;
Richardson 1963
;
Ibañez et al. 2009
;
Claeys et al. 2016
. To this end, we introduced a broad family of flat band Hubbard models constructed within the framework of topological quantum chemistry. Their groundstates are bosonic condensates featuring maximal off-diagonal long-range order at half-filling and a linear-in-
|U|
pairing gap. Computing the mass of the condensing Cooper pair, we established that symmetry-protected wavefunction topology coheres the superconductor with a nonzero superfluid weight. We also found exact Cooper pair bound states with
p,d,\dots
-wave symmetries below the pairing gap and completely classified Cooper pair bands with fragile topology. Finally, we solved the Goldstone and gapped spin-wave excitations which hint at BEC-like behavior. Further study of these exactly solvable models may provide a new route to high-temperature superconductivity.

the ONR Grant No. N00014-20-1-2303,
the Schmidt Fund for Innovative Research,
Simons Investigator Grant No. 404513,
the Packard Foundation,
the Gordon and Betty Moore Foundation through the EPiQS Initiative, Grant GBMF11070 and Grant No. GBMF8685 towards the Princeton theory program.
Further support was provided by the NSF-MRSEC Grant No. DMR-2011750,
BSF Israel US foundation Grant No. 2018226,
and the Princeton Global Network Funds.

Appendix A
Single-Particle Symmetry and Topology

In this section, we define the notation used in the rest of the work for the single-particle projectors, projected many-body operators, and space group symmetries (App.
A.1
). We prove
that the uniform pairing condition can be symmetry enforced by choosing orbitals to transform as irreps of a single Wyckoff positions (App.
A.2
), and extend this result to bipartite lattices where perfectly flat bands can be systematically constructed.

A.1
Projected Bands

Our problem projects the main electronic degrees of freedom into the quasi-flat bands of the non-interacting Hamiltonian. In a strong coupling approximation, the quasi-flat bands are set to zero energy and the remaining bands are sent to infinite energy and projected out. As such, the physics depends entirely on the flat band wavefunctions.

We now introduce our notation which differs slightly from Ref.
[
18
]
where only nondegenerate flat bands (doubly degenerate when including spin) were considered. Let
\mathbf{R}
index the unit cells and
\alpha=1,\dots,N_{orb}
index the orbitals at positions
\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}
. For now we neglect spin, but it will be introduced later with
\sigma=\uparrow,\downarrow
. The electron operators (obeying canonical anti-commutation relations) are

\displaystyle c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha})}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha},\qquad c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha})}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}

(16)

where
\mathcal{N}
is the total number of unit cells, which is the number of terms in the
\mathbf{R},\mathbf{k}
sums. The general single-particle Hamiltonian reads

\displaystyle\tilde{H}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k},\alpha\beta}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}\tilde{h}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k},\beta}=\sum_{\mathbf{k},n}\tilde{E}_{n}(\mathbf{k})\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n},\qquad\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha}[\tilde{U}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})]_{n,\alpha}c_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}

(17)

and
\tilde{h}(\mathbf{k})=\tilde{U}\tilde{E}\tilde{U}^{\dagger}
where
\tilde{E}
is the diagonal matrix of energies,
\tilde{U}
is the
N_{orb}\times N_{orb}
eigenvector matrix, and the diagonalized electron operators obey

\{\gamma_{\mathbf{k},m},\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n}\}=\delta_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}[\tilde{U}^{\dagger}\tilde{U}]_{mn}=\delta_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{mn}
. For brevity, we often suppress the
\mathbf{k}
dependence.

We assume there is a gap around the
N_{f}
quasi-flat bands, which we will refer to as the flat bands; in the strong coupling approximation, we do not require them to be exactly flat. We project all operators into the flat bands by setting
\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n}=0
if
n
is not one of the flat bands. Now denote
U(\mathbf{k})
as the
N_{orb}\times N_{f}
occupied eigenstate matrix which obeys
U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})U(\mathbf{k})=\mathbb{1}_{N_{f}}
and
U(\mathbf{k})U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})=P(\mathbf{k})
where
P
is the
N_{orb}\times N_{orb}
projector matrix obeying
P^{2}=P,P^{\dagger}=P,\text{Tr}\ P=N_{f}
. We recall that in the exactly flat case, the eigenstates are degenerate and hence are only defined up to the gauge freedom
U(\mathbf{k})\to U(\mathbf{k})\mathcal{W}(\mathbf{k})
where
\mathcal{W}(\mathbf{k})\in U(N_{f})
is a unitary matrix with arbitrary
\mathbf{k}
dependence
[
19
]
. Both
U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})U(\mathbf{k})
and
U(\mathbf{k})U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})
are invariant under this gauge freedom. The projector
P(\mathbf{k})
encodes the topology and quantum geometry of the bulk bands. It will play an essential role in the many-body problem, highlighting the interplay of topology and interactions.

We now define the projected operators. Because we have set all
\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n}
to zero other than the flat band operators, an (over)complete basis of the projected states is given by

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}\equiv\sum_{n=1}^{N_{orb}}[\tilde{U}(\mathbf{k})]_{\alpha,n}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N_{f}}[U(\mathbf{k})]_{\alpha,n}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n}=\sum_{\beta}[U(\mathbf{k})U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})]_{\alpha\beta}c_{\mathbf{k},\beta}=\sum_{\beta}[P(\mathbf{k})]_{\alpha\beta}c_{\mathbf{k},\beta}

(18)

using Eq. (
17
). Because the projector
P(\mathbf{k})
is Hermitian, we have

\displaystyle\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\beta}P^{*}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})=\sum_{\beta}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\beta}P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\ .

(19)

The
\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}
basis is invariant under the eigenvector gauge freedom (unlike the
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}
operators). However, the barred operators do not obey canonical anti-commutation relations because they are an overcomplete basis, whereas the
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}
operators form a complete basis. We compute

\displaystyle\{\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha},\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\beta}\}=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}P_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})\{c_{\mathbf{k},\alpha^{\prime}},c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\}P_{\beta^{\prime}\beta}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})=\delta_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}[P(\mathbf{k})^{2}]_{\alpha\beta}=\delta_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})\ .

(20)

We now need the projected position operators in real space. They are defined in analogy to Eq. (
16
) as

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha})}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}

(21)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\beta}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha})}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k},\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\beta}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}-\mathbf{r}_{\beta})}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})\right)c_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\beta}p_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})c_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\beta}

where we defined the Fourier transform of the projector, i.e. the real-space correlation function of the non-interacting flat bands, as

\displaystyle p_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}-\mathbf{r}_{\beta})}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})}[V(\mathbf{k})P(\mathbf{k})V^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})]_{\alpha\beta},\quad[V(\mathbf{k})]_{\alpha\beta}=e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\ .

(22)

The embedding matrix
V(\mathbf{k})
defined in Eq. (
22
) is a unitary diagonal matrix. Although it is sometimes useful
[
19
]
to take the thermodynamic limit
\mathcal{N}\to\infty
, we will keep
\mathcal{N}
finite throughout this work.

We prove some properties about
p(\mathbf{R})
. It is obvious that
p(\mathbf{R})=p^{\dagger}(-\mathbf{R})
by Hermiticity of
P(\mathbf{k})
and
\text{Tr}\ p(\mathbf{R})=N_{f}\delta_{\mathbf{R},0}
follows from
\text{Tr}\ P(\mathbf{k})=N_{f}
. We also have the Fourier inversion

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{k})=\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}V^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})p(\mathbf{R})V(\mathbf{k})\ .

(23)

From the projector property
P^{2}=P
, we find the convolution identity
[
19
]

\displaystyle p(\mathbf{R})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}p(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})p(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})

(24)

which we use to derive the real space anti-commutation relations (and can also be recovered from Eq.
20
). We compute

\displaystyle\{\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha},\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\beta}\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{S}\mathbf{S}^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}p_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{S})[p^{\dagger}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}-\mathbf{S}^{\prime})]_{\beta^{\prime}\beta}\{c_{\mathbf{S},\alpha^{\prime}},c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{S}^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\}

(25)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{S}}[p(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{S})p^{\dagger}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}-\mathbf{S})]_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{S}}[p(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+\mathbf{S})p^{\dagger}(\mathbf{S})]_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=p_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})\ .

In the thermodynamic limit where
\mathcal{N}\to\infty
, the flat bands are gapped so
P(\mathbf{k})
is continuous on the BZ and the real space correlation function
p(\mathbf{R})
decays at least exponentially fast.

We now look at the projected density operators
\bar{n}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}=\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}
. We use the anti-commutation relations Eq. (
20
), finding

\displaystyle\bar{n}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}^{2}

\displaystyle=\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}=\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}P_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}-\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}=P_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\bar{n}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}

(26)

where the second term vanishes because
\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}
anti-commutes with itself. In real space, the density
\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}=\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}
obeys

\displaystyle\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}^{2}

\displaystyle=\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}=\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}p_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R})\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}-\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}=p_{\alpha\alpha}(0)\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}

(27)

using Eq. (
25
). The correlation function elements
p_{\alpha\alpha}(0)
are onsite and measure local density of each orbital. They will play an important role in the many-body problem.

One may also derive these expressions from the electron operators in the kinetic energy band basis,
\gamma_{{\bf{k}},m}
. In this basis, projection simply involves restricting the bands to
m\in M
, where
M
labels the low-energy flat bands. The projected density reads

\displaystyle{\bar{n}}_{{\bf{k}},\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n}U({\bf{k}})^{*}_{\alpha,m}U({\bf{k}})_{\alpha,n}\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}},m}\gamma_{{\bf{k}},n}

(28)

\displaystyle{\bar{n}}_{{\bf{k}},\alpha}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n}U({\bf{k}})^{*}_{\alpha,m}U({\bf{k}})_{\alpha,n}\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}},m}\gamma_{{\bf{k}},n}\sum_{m^{\prime},n^{\prime}}U({\bf{k}})^{*}_{\alpha,m^{\prime}}U({\bf{k}})_{\alpha,n^{\prime}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}},m^{\prime}}\gamma_{{\bf{k}},n^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n}U({\bf{k}})^{*}_{\alpha,m}U({\bf{k}})_{\alpha,n}\sum_{m^{\prime},n^{\prime}}U({\bf{k}})^{*}_{\alpha,m^{\prime}}U({\bf{k}})_{\alpha,n^{\prime}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}},m}[\{\gamma_{{\bf{k}},n},\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}},m^{\prime}}\}-\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}},m^{\prime}}\gamma_{{\bf{k}},n}]\gamma_{{\bf{k}},n^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n}U({\bf{k}})^{*}_{\alpha,m}U({\bf{k}})_{\alpha,n}\sum_{m^{\prime},n^{\prime}}U({\bf{k}})^{*}_{\alpha,m^{\prime}}U({\bf{k}})_{\alpha,n^{\prime}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}},m}[\delta_{n,m^{\prime}}-0]\gamma_{{\bf{k}},n^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=P_{\alpha\alpha}({\bf{k}}){\bar{n}}_{{\bf{k}},\alpha}.

(29)

The zero in the second to last line arises from the antisymmetry upon interchange of the
m,m^{\prime}
indices.

A.2
Uniform Pairing Condition from the Space group

As Tovmasyan, Peotta, Törmä, and Huber identified in Ref.
[
18
]
, the solvability of the Hubbard model groundstates and excitations hinges on enforcing a simple criterion called the
uniform pairing condition
(UPC):
\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha}\bar{n}^{2}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\propto\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}+\bar{N}
where
\bar{N}
is the total (projected) number operator. The UPC was originally proposed to ensure equal mean-field pairing strength on all the orbital sites, but the UPC is simply a property of the single-particle wavefunctions as we will show. Using Eq. (
27
), we expand
\bar{n}^{2}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}
to find

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha}\bar{n}^{2}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}=\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha}p_{\alpha\alpha}(0)\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}

(30)

To enforce Eq. (
30
), we must have

\displaystyle p_{\alpha\alpha}(0)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=\epsilon\text{ if }\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\neq 0,~~\forall\alpha

(31)

where the constant
\epsilon
is independent of
\alpha
and is fixed by
\text{Tr}\ p(0)=\sum_{\alpha}p_{\alpha\alpha}(0)=N_{f}
. We will derive the UPC (Eq. (
30
)) from the appearance of the eta-pairing symmetry in the following section App.
B
.

The case of
\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}=0
can occur for a given
\alpha
at all
\mathbf{R}
if the wavefunction of the flat bands is exactly zero on the orbital
\alpha
(see Eq. (
19
)). We will see that this is the case in the bipartite crystalline lattices we consider shortly
[
34
]
.

We now show that space group symmetries can enforce the uniform pairing condition
generically
: arbitrary hoppings (preserving
G
) can be chosen as long as the underlying orbitals obey simple symmetry conditions which we make precise shortly. We consider unitary space group symmetries
g\in G,\ g=T_{\boldsymbol{\delta}}g_{s}
consisting of a symmorphic part
g_{s}
(a rotation or reflection) and non-symmorphic part
T_{\boldsymbol{\delta}}
(a translation by a fraction of a lattice vector). The vector representation of
g
on real-space vectors
\mathbf{r}
is defined to be
g\mathbf{r}=g_{s}\mathbf{r}+\boldsymbol{\delta}

[
109
]
. The representations in momentum space take the form
D[g]=e^{ig_{s}\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\delta}}D[g_{s}]
where
D[g_{s}]
is the
N_{orb}\times N_{orb}
representation on the orbitals. On the single-particle Hamiltonian,
g
enforces

\displaystyle e^{ig_{s}\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\delta}}D[g_{s}]\tilde{h}(\mathbf{k})\left(e^{ig_{s}\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\delta}}D[g_{s}]\right)^{\dagger}=D[g_{s}]\tilde{h}(\mathbf{k})D^{\dagger}[g_{s}]=\tilde{h}(g_{s}\mathbf{k}),

(32)

canceling the non-symmorphic phases. From Eq. (
32
),
D[g_{s}]P(\mathbf{k})D^{\dagger}[g_{s}]=P(g_{s}\mathbf{k})
[
19
,
109
]
. It is understood that the representation matrix
D[g]
only depends on the symmorphic part of
g
, and henceforth we drop the
s
subscript.

We also need some simple notions from topological quantum chemistry. In a lattice with space group
G
, a Wyckoff position
[
27
,
28
]

x
of multiplicity
n
consists of the points
\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n}
in the unit cell which form an orbit of
G
: there exists
g\in G
such that
\mathbf{x}_{i}=g\mathbf{x}_{j}
for each
\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}\in x
. The site symmetry group of a point
\mathbf{x}
is defined by
G_{\mathbf{x}}=\{g\in G|g\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}\}\in G
, and all
G_{\mathbf{x}}
for
\mathbf{x}\in x
are isomorphic. Hence in any lattice with
G
, we can organize the orbitals into irreps of
G_{x}
at some Wyckoff position
x
. All such irreps are tabulated on the
Bilbao Crystallographic Server

[
29
,
30
]
. For instance in
G=p2
generated by
C_{2}
rotation and translations, the
1a
position (of multiplicity 1) is the origin of the unit cell and
G_{1a}=\{1,C_{2}\}
with two 1D irreps (even and odd under
C_{2}
). The
2e
position (of multiplicity 2) is the generic nonmaximal position consisting of a pair of
C_{2}
-related points, so
G_{2e}
is the trivial group
\{1\}
(see Fig.
2
).

Figure 2:
Unit cell of the wallpaper group
p2
generated by
C_{2}
and translations (we take
\mathbf{a}_{1}=(1,0),\mathbf{a}_{2}=(0,1)
for ease). There are four maximal Wyckoff positons 1a =
(0,0)
,1b=
(0,1/2)
,1c=
(0,1/2)
,1d=
(1/2,1/2)
which all have multiplicity 1 and site symmetry group
G_{x}=2
. At 1a for instance, the site symmetry group is
\{1,C_{2}\}
. The non-maximal Wyckoff position 2e
=\{(x,y),(1-x,1-y)\}
has multiplicity 2 and consists of two general inversion-related points in the unit cell. Since
C_{2}
exchanges the two points in 2e, it is not part of the site symmetry group and thus
G_{2e}=1
containing only the identity.

We can now state our claim.

Lemma
. Let
x
be a Wyckoff position (not necessarily maximal) with site-symmetry group
G_{x}
and irreps
\chi
. The uniform pairing condition holds between all orbitals forming an irrep
\chi
induced from the Wyckoff position
x
.

Proof.
We define
\tilde{G}
as the group of symmetries of the unit cell, i.e. the space group mod lattice translations. We define the site-symmetry group
H=G_{\mathbf{x}_{1}}\cong G_{x}
where for concreteness
G_{\mathbf{x}_{1}}
is the point group of the site
\mathbf{x}_{1}\in x
. For a Wyckoff position of multiplicity
n
and an irrep
\chi
of
G_{x}
with dimension
|\chi|
and (irreducible) representations
D^{\chi}[h]
for
h\in H
, the Wyckoff position contains
n|\chi|
orbitals per unit cell. We index the orbitals in a tensor product basis
\alpha=i,a
where
i=1,\dots,n
and
a=1,\dots,|\chi|
. It is useful to note that in this basis, the embedding matrices are block diagonal,
V_{ia,jb}[\mathbf{G}]=e^{-i\mathbf{r}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{G}}\delta_{ij}\delta_{ab}
. It is natural to employ a coset decomposition separating the onsite symmetries of
H
and the symmetries that connect different points in the Wyckoff position. The formal expression is
\tilde{G}=\tilde{g}_{1}H+\dots+\tilde{g}_{n}H
,
n=\frac{|\tilde{G}|}{|H|}
such that irreps of
H
form an induced representation in
\tilde{G}

[
56
]
. Hence the representation matrices of
G
can be chosen as

\displaystyle D[h]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{1}_{n}\otimes D^{\chi}[h],\quad h\in H

(33)

\displaystyle D[\tilde{g}_{i}]

\displaystyle=P_{\tilde{g}_{i}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{|\chi|},\qquad\tilde{g}_{i}\in\{\tilde{g}_{1},\dots,\tilde{g}_{n}\}

where
[P_{\tilde{g}}]_{ij}=\delta_{\tilde{g}\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}}
is a permutation matrix
[
56
]
on the
n
sites of the Wyckoff position, and the Kronecker delta is understood mod lattice vectors. First we prove uniform pairing holds within the
|\chi|
orbitals at a given
\mathbf{x}_{i}
.
Choose an arbitrary diagonal block of
P(\mathbf{k})
denoted
[P_{i}(\mathbf{k})]_{ab}=P_{ia,ib}(\mathbf{k})
. We have denoted
\alpha=ia
and
\beta=ib
. Note that
P_{i}(\mathbf{k})=P_{i}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G})
because the embedding matrix acts as
V_{ia,ib}[\mathbf{G}]=e^{-i\mathbf{x}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{G}}\delta_{ab}
, proportional to the identity. Then because the BZ is symmetric under
H
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{i}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{i}(h\mathbf{k})=D^{\chi}[h]\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{i}(\mathbf{k})\right)D^{\chi}[h]^{\dagger}

(34)

but because
D^{\chi}[h]
is irreducible, by Schur’s lemma we find
\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{i}(\mathbf{k})=\epsilon_{i}\mathbb{1}_{|\chi|}
where
\epsilon_{i}
is an arbitrary constant. That is, the only matrix invariant under an irreducible representation is proportional to the identity. If there were another invariant matrix, call it
M
, that matrix
M
would commute with all symmetries and thus will block-diagonalize the representation matrices, implying the representation was reducible.

To prove that
\epsilon_{i}=\epsilon_{j}
, relating different blocks, we use the fact that there exists
\tilde{g}\notin H
such that
\tilde{g}\mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{x}_{j}
. Then because
D[\tilde{g}]P(\mathbf{k})D^{\dagger}[\tilde{g}]=P(\tilde{g}\mathbf{k})
, we find that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{i}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{i}(g\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{j}(\mathbf{k})

(35)

such that
\epsilon_{i}=\epsilon_{j}
. Thus
\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=\epsilon
for all
\alpha
in a the irrep
\chi
of the Wyckoff position
x
. Because
\text{Tr}\ P(\mathbf{k})=N_{f}
, the number of projected bands, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\frac{1}{n|\chi|}\text{Tr}\ P(\mathbf{k})=\frac{N_{f}}{n|\chi|}\equiv\epsilon\leq 1.

(36)

The value of
\epsilon
has physical meaning. From Eq. (
23
), we see that
\epsilon=p_{\alpha\alpha}(0)
, which is the diagonal of the onsite correlation function. Although we have discussed only unitary symmetry groups in this proof, anti-unitary spatial symmetries, which do not act on spin, also enforce uniform pairing. This is easily seen because
P(\mathbf{k})
is Hermitian and its diagonal elements must be real, so complex conjugation acts trivially. In App.
F
, we use the spinless symmetry
C_{6z}T
to enforce uniform pairing between 6 orbitals.

We now extend this result to the case of a bipartite crystalline lattice consisting of the sublattices
L
and
\tilde{L}
. The integers
N_{L}
and
N_{\tilde{L}}
denote the number of orbitals on each sublattice, so
N_{orb}=N_{L}+N_{\tilde{L}}
. Without loss of generality we take
N_{L}>N_{\tilde{L}}
. We consider single-particle Hamiltonians of the form

\displaystyle\tilde{h}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&S_{\mathbf{k}}\\
S_{\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}&B_{\mathbf{k}}\end{pmatrix}

(37)

where
S_{\mathbf{k}}
is a
N_{L}\times N_{\tilde{L}}
rectangular matrix and
B_{\mathbf{k}}
is an
N_{\tilde{L}}\times N_{\tilde{L}}
Hermitian matrix. The number of flat bands is
N_{f}=N_{L}-N_{\tilde{L}}>0
and their wavefunctions obey
[
34
]

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&S_{\mathbf{k}}\\
S_{\mathbf{k}}^{\dagger}&B_{\mathbf{k}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{\mathbf{k}}\\
0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
S^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}}\psi_{\mathbf{k}}\end{pmatrix}=0

(38)

where the existence of
N_{f}=N_{L}-N_{\tilde{L}}
wavefunctions
\psi_{\mathbf{k},n},n=1,\dots,N_{f}
in the nullspace of
S^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}}
is guaranteed by rank deficiency. Thus the flat band projector

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N_{f}}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n}&0\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{n=1}^{N_{f}}\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}\psi^{{\dagger}}_{\mathbf{k},n}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

(39)

has nonzero entries only in the
L
sublattice. As such, the projected operators
\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}=P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k},\beta}
are only nonzero when
\alpha
is an orbital in the
L
sublattice, and so
\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha\in\tilde{L}}=0
. Hence for Eq. (
30
) to hold, we only require
p_{\alpha\alpha}(0)
to be independent of
\alpha
within the
L
sublattice. As proven in the Lemma above, this is accomplished by requiring the
L
sublattice to consist of orbitals within a single irrep. More than one irrep would no longer guarantee the uniform pairing condition. Again because
\text{Tr}\ p(0)=N_{f}=N_{L}-N_{\tilde{L}}
, we have

\displaystyle p_{\alpha\alpha}(0)=\frac{N_{f}}{N_{L}}\equiv\epsilon<1\ .

(40)

For simplicity of notation, we will not distinguish between sums over
\alpha\in L\oplus\tilde{L}
and
\alpha\in L
. This is because the flat band wavefunctions have no weight in the
\tilde{L}
sublattice, and so the sums are interchangable (any additional terms belonging to
\tilde{L}
are
0
).

The notable feature of our construction is the total freedom to choose the hopping elements once the space group and orbitals are fixed. Using the bipartite crystalline lattice construction, we obtain perfectly flat bands obeying the uniform pairing condition in all space groups. The topology of the flat bands can be determined directly as the formal subtraction of the band representations of the two sublattices, and in many cases is fragile topological or obstructed atomic
[
34
]
. If the formal subtraction results in a “negative” irrep at high symmetry momentum
\bf K
, then the flat bands are gapless with the dispersive bands at
\bf K
. In this case, projection into the flat bands is not justified, but this case can be analyzed very generally in mean field theory
[
61
]
.

Ref.
[
34
]
also constructs auxiliary models from the bipartite Hamiltonian in Eq. (
37
). By integrating out the
\tilde{L}
sublattice, one obtains the positive semi-definite
N_{L}\times N_{L}
Hamiltonian
\tilde{h}_{L}(\mathbf{k})=S_{\mathbf{k}}S^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}}
which retains the
N_{L}-N_{\tilde{L}}
zero-energy flat bands with wavefunctions
\psi_{\mathbf{k},n}
, as well as
N_{\tilde{L}}
dispersive bands. These models
\tilde{h}_{L}(\mathbf{k})
also possess the uniform pairing condition.

A.2.1
Spin

To add spin, we impose
S_{z}
conservation and spinful time-reversal symmetry
\mathcal{T}
. Together,
S_{z}
and
\mathcal{T}
ensure the existence of an eta-pairing operator as we show in App.
B
. Enforcing these symmetries, the single-particle Hamiltonian takes the form

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{s}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{h}_{\uparrow}(\mathbf{k})&\\
&\tilde{h}_{\downarrow}(\mathbf{k})\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{h}(\mathbf{k})&\\
&\tilde{h}^{*}(-\mathbf{k})\\
\end{pmatrix}

(41)

where we used
D[\mathcal{T}]=i\sigma_{y}K
, so in particular
\mathcal{T}
acts diagonally on the orbital index
\alpha,\beta
. Here
\sigma_{y}
acts on the spin indices and
K
is complex conjugation. Because of the global symmetry
S_{z}
, we can fix the gauge of
\mathcal{T}
on the eigenstates so that
U^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{k})=U^{\uparrow}(-\mathbf{k})^{*}
and
P^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{k})=P^{\uparrow}(-\mathbf{k})^{*}
. As such we have

\displaystyle p_{\alpha\beta}^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}-\mathbf{r}_{\beta})}P_{\alpha\beta}^{\uparrow}(-\mathbf{k})^{*}=\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime}-\mathbf{r}_{\beta})}P_{\alpha\beta}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{k})\right)^{*}=p^{\uparrow}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})^{*}

(42)

which is just the statement that
\mathcal{T}
acts locally in real space. If the uniform pairing condition is obeyed by
p^{\downarrow}(0)
, it is also by
p^{\uparrow}(0)
using
\mathcal{T}
and
S_{z}
.

Appendix B
Hubbard Model and Eta-pairing Symmetry

In this section, we construct a class of positive semi-definite attractive Hubbard models using the uniform pairing condition generalizing the model of Ref.
[
18
]
(App.
B.1
). The existence of an eta-pairing symmetry in the models we consider is protected by
S_{z}
and
\mathcal{T}
. We derive the form of the eta-pairing symmetry (App.
B.2
) and show that it expands the
U(1)\times U(1)
charge-spin symmetry group to a non-abelian
SU(2)
symmetry group (App.
B.3
).

B.1
Attractive Hubbard Models

We now add a Hubbard term
H
to the non-interacting Hamiltonian
\tilde{H}
in Eq. (
17
). The form of the Hubbard term was discovered in Ref.
[
18
]
and yields an eta-pairing symmetry,
[H,\eta]=0
. The first step is to define a projected Hermitian spin operator

\displaystyle\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}

\displaystyle=\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}-\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}=\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}-\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}\ .

(43)

With the uniform pairing condition Eq. (
31
), the form of this operator naturally gives an attractive Hubbard model. We define

\displaystyle H=\frac{1}{2}|U|\sum_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}(\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha})^{2}\ .

(44)

The crucial feature of
H
is that it is the sum of squares and thus is positive semi-definite. Hence a state
\ket{\psi}
obeying
H\ket{\psi}=0
must be a ground state. Now we show that
H
is an attractive Hubbard model. Using Eq. (
27
), we compute

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|U|\sum_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}(\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}-\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow})^{2}

(45)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}|U|\sum_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}-2\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}+p^{\uparrow}_{\alpha\alpha}(0)\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}+p^{\downarrow}_{\alpha\alpha}(0)\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}

\displaystyle=-|U|\sum_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}+\frac{\epsilon|U|}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}+\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}

\displaystyle=-|U|\sum_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}+\frac{\epsilon|U|}{2}\bar{N}

which is a Hubbard interaction with strength
|U|
and a chemical potential term
\frac{\epsilon|U|}{2}\bar{N}
. To get from line 2 to line 3, we have employed the uniform pairing condition
p^{\uparrow}_{\alpha\alpha}(0)=p^{\downarrow}_{\alpha\alpha}(0)=\frac{\epsilon|U|}{2}
. We have also used the fact that

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha})}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\alpha,\sigma}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k}\alpha,\sigma}=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha,\sigma}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}\alpha,\sigma}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k}\alpha,\sigma}=\sum_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma}=\bar{N}

(46)

where the final sum is over the
N_{f}
flat bands. Let us make a brief comment about the form of the attractive Hubbard term. The form of
\sum_{\alpha}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}
is somewhat special because each orbital
\alpha
only couples to itself (but opposite in spin). If there is only one orbital per Wyckoff position (i.e. a 1D irrep of
G_{x}
), this is the same type of term that one would get from a purely local Coulomb interaction. However if the irrep
\chi
is multi-dimensional, like a pair of
p_{x},p_{y}
orbitals per site, then a Coulomb interaction coupling the
total
densities would be
\sum_{i}(\sum_{a}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},ia,\uparrow})(\sum_{a}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},ia,\downarrow})
in the tensor product notation of App.
A.2
where
a
indexes the orbitals at a given point
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in x
. Given that the mechanism of generating attractive Hubbard interactions is typically phonons with a complicated coupling structure, we view Eq. (
45
) as a simple model capturing the essential physics. Although we focus here on an attractive interaction, a particle-hole transformation
[
110
]
on the
\downarrow
spins maps all our results to the repulsive case with ferromagnetism.

B.2
Eta Operators

Generically, the
\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}
operators do not commute among themselves because of the projection (see Eq. (
25
)). However, in the trivial atomic limit case where the orbitals are totally decoupled,
\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\to S_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}
is commuting for all
\mathbf{R},\alpha
, yielding an infinite number of conserved charges. This is the classical limit where the model contains only strictly local density operators. This limit does not have superconductivity because there is no coherence – every site is decoupled. This is an indication that some obstruction to atomic localization, like topology or symmetry protected obstructed states, is necessary for the projected strong-coupling model to have a superconducting phase.

There is still a nontrivial symmetry operator when the projected bands are not atomic, the eta-pairing symmetry
[
18
]
. We use the most general two-body, charge
+2
, spin 0, momentum 0 operator for our ansatz of the
\eta^{\dagger}
operator. It takes the form

\displaystyle\eta^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{q}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},m,\uparrow}\Omega_{mn}(\mathbf{q})\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},n,\downarrow}

(47)

involving the yet undetermined matrix
\Omega_{mn}(\mathbf{q})
. We now must discuss the invariance of
\eta^{\dagger}
under the eigenvector gauge freedom
U^{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})\to U^{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})\mathcal{W}^{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})
. Clearly for
\eta^{\dagger}
to be a physical symmetry, it must be invariant under the eigenvector gauge freedom which severely restricts the form of
\eta
to a simple ansatz. We will find that with
S_{z}
and
\mathcal{T}
, this ansatz is successful. Since
S_{z}
and
\mathcal{T}
are the symmetries relevant for real materials, we only consider this case explicitly in our work. However, we mention that the combination of particle-hole symmetry
\mathcal{P}
and
SU(2)
spin symmetry also protects the existence of an
\eta
operator.

The operators
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},n,\sigma}
are not gauge invariant. Under the eigenvector gauge freedom, we have
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},n,\sigma}\to\sum_{n^{\prime}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},n^{\prime},\sigma}\mathcal{W}^{\sigma}_{n^{\prime}n}(\mathbf{q})
where
\mathcal{W}^{\sigma}(\mathbf{q})
is a unitary matrix. Hence

\displaystyle\eta^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\to\sum_{\mathbf{q},mnm^{\prime}n^{\prime}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},m^{\prime},\uparrow}\mathcal{W}_{m^{\prime}m}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{q})\Omega_{mn}(\mathbf{q})\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},n^{\prime},\downarrow}\mathcal{W}_{n^{\prime}n}^{\downarrow}(-\mathbf{q})

(48)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{q},m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},m^{\prime},\uparrow}[\mathcal{W}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{q})\Omega(\mathbf{q})\mathcal{W}^{\downarrow{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q})^{*}]_{m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},n^{\prime},\downarrow}\ .

In order for
\eta^{\dagger}
to be invariant, we must have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{q})\Omega(\mathbf{q})\mathcal{W}^{\downarrow{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q})^{*}

\displaystyle=\Omega(\mathbf{q})\ .

(49)

The natural object
\Omega
transforming in this way is a sewing matrix
[
111
,
112
]
relating states at
\mathbf{q}
and
-\mathbf{q}
. Because of the conjugation in Eq. (
49
), the sewing matrix must be that of an anti-unitary symmetry, like time-reversal.

Fixing the gauge of the
S_{z}
and
\mathcal{T}
sewing matrices so that
U^{\uparrow}(-\mathbf{q})^{*}=U^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{q})
, we can choose eigenstates such that
\mathcal{W}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{q})=\mathcal{W}^{\downarrow}(-\mathbf{q})^{*}
and thus
\mathcal{W}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{q})\Omega(\mathbf{q})\mathcal{W}^{\downarrow{\dagger}}(-\mathbf{q})^{*}=\mathcal{W}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{q})\Omega(\mathbf{q})\mathcal{W}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{q})^{\dagger}
. Thus
\eta^{\dagger}
is invariant under the gauge freedom if
\mathcal{W}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{q})\Omega(\mathbf{q})\mathcal{W}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{q})^{\dagger}=\Omega(\mathbf{q})
which is satisfied for all arbitrary
\mathcal{W}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{q})
iff
\Omega_{mn}(\mathbf{q})\propto\delta_{mn}
. Thus we arrive at the ansatz

\displaystyle\eta^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{q},m}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},m,\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},m,\downarrow}

(50)

in the gauge-fixed wavefunctions where
U^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{k})=U^{\downarrow}(-\mathbf{k})^{*}
. The
\eta^{\dagger}
operator takes a simpler form in terms of the projected operators (which are gauge invariant). Recalling
\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},m,\sigma}=\sum_{\alpha}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},\alpha,\sigma}U^{\sigma}_{\alpha,m}(\mathbf{q})
, we have

\displaystyle\eta^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{q},m\alpha\beta}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},\alpha,\uparrow}U^{\uparrow}_{\alpha m}(\mathbf{q})U^{\downarrow}_{\beta m}(-\mathbf{q})\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},\beta,\downarrow}=\sum_{\mathbf{q},\alpha\beta}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},\alpha,\uparrow}P^{\uparrow}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{q})\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},\beta,\downarrow}=\sum_{\mathbf{q}\alpha}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},\alpha,\downarrow}=\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}.

(51)

In the last equality of Eq. (
51
), we Fourier transformed to real space. Physically,
\eta^{\dagger}
can be understood as creating a Cooper pair at zero momentum. Note that the wavefunction of
\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}
decays exponentially outside the correlation length of
p(\mathbf{R})
, which describes the “size” of the Cooper pair bound state.

We now proceed to the commutator of
\eta^{\dagger}
and
\bar{S}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}
:

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha},\eta^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\beta}[\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}-\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow},\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\beta,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\beta,\downarrow}]

(52)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\beta}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}[\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow},\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\beta,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\beta,\downarrow}]-\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}[\bar{c}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow},\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\beta,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\beta,\downarrow}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\beta}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}p_{\alpha\beta}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\beta,\downarrow}+\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\beta,\uparrow}p_{\alpha\beta}^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})

where we used the anti-commutator
\{\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma},\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\beta,\sigma^{\prime}}\}=\delta_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})\delta_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}
(see Eq. (
25
)). We expand the
\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\beta,\sigma}
operators using Eq. (
21
) to find

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha},\eta^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta\beta^{\prime}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime},\downarrow}p_{\alpha\beta}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})p^{\downarrow*}_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime})+\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime},\uparrow}p_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}^{\uparrow*}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime})p_{\alpha\beta}^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})

(53)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta\beta^{\prime}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime},\downarrow}p_{\alpha\beta}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})p^{\uparrow}_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime})+\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime},\uparrow}p_{\beta\beta^{\prime}}^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime})p_{\alpha\beta}^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime},\downarrow}[p^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})p^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime})]_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}+\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime},\uparrow}[p^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})p^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime})]_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}

where we used
p^{\uparrow}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R})^{*}=p^{\downarrow}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R})
thanks to
\mathcal{T}
in App.
A.2.1
. Finally, the convolution identity of
p(\mathbf{R})
in Eq. (
24
) gives

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha},\eta^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime},\downarrow}p_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime})+\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime},\uparrow}p_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime})

(54)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime},\downarrow}p_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}^{\downarrow*}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime})+\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime},\uparrow}p_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}^{\uparrow*}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=(\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}+\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow})

\displaystyle=0

again using Eq. (
21
) to recover the projected operators in the penultimate line. Thus we have proven
\eta^{\dagger}
commutes with
\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}
, so it is trivially established that
[H,\eta^{\dagger}]=0
. It follows that the eta-pairing states are all eigenstates:

\displaystyle H\eta^{{\dagger}n}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\eta^{{\dagger}n}H\ket{0}=0

(55)

and in particular must be groundstates because
H
is positive semi-definite. Note that because
[\eta^{\dagger},\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}]=0
, one could consider much more general (though not necessarily physical) Hamiltonians with an eta-pairing symmetry than the onsite
H=\frac{|U|}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}
Hamiltonian studied in this work.

One may also calculate the commutators using the
\gamma
basis. We define the momentum space spin operator

\displaystyle\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{q},\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot{\bf{R}}}\bar{S}^{z}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha}

(56)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\sigma}(-1)^{\sigma}e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot{\bf{R}}}{\bar{n}}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\sigma}

(57)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bf{k}},\alpha,\sigma}(-1)^{\sigma}{\bar{c}}^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}}-{\bf{q}},\alpha,\sigma}{\bar{c}}_{{\bf{k}},\alpha,\sigma}

(58)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bf{k}},\alpha,\sigma}(-1)^{\sigma}[U^{*}({\bf{k}}-{\bf{q}})]_{\alpha,m,\sigma}[U({\bf{k}})]_{\alpha,n,\sigma}\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}}-{\bf{q}},m,\sigma}\gamma_{{\bf{k}},n,\sigma}.

(59)

The commutator becomes

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\bar{S}^{z}_{{\bf{p}},\alpha},\eta^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bf{k}},m,n,\sigma}\sum_{\mathbf{q},l}[(-1)^{\sigma}[U^{*}({\bf{k}}-{\bf{p}})]_{\alpha,m,\sigma}[U({\bf{k}})]_{\alpha,n,\sigma}\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}}-{\bf{p}},m,\sigma}\gamma_{{\bf{k}},n,\sigma},\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},l,\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},l,\downarrow}]

(60)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bf{k}},m,n,\sigma}\sum_{\mathbf{q},l}[U^{*}({\bf{k}}-{\bf{p}})]_{\alpha,m,\sigma}[U({\bf{k}})]_{\alpha,n,\sigma}\left(\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}}-{\bf{p}},m,\uparrow}\delta_{{\bf{k}},\mathbf{q}}\delta_{ln}\delta_{\sigma,\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},l,\downarrow}-\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}}-{\bf{p}},m,\downarrow}\delta_{{\bf{k}},-\mathbf{q}}\delta_{ln}\delta_{\sigma,\downarrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},l,\uparrow}\right)

(61)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bf{k}},m,n,\sigma}[U^{*}({\bf{k}}-{\bf{p}})]_{\alpha,m,\sigma}[U({\bf{k}})]_{\alpha,n,\sigma}\left(\delta_{\sigma,\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}}-{\bf{p}},m,\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-{\bf{k}},n,\downarrow}-\delta_{\sigma,\downarrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}}-{\bf{p}},m,\downarrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-{\bf{k}},n,\uparrow}\right)

(62)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bf{k}},m,n,\sigma}\left([U^{*}({\bf{k}}-{\bf{p}})]_{\uparrow,m,\sigma}[U({\bf{k}})]_{\uparrow,n,\sigma}-[U^{*}(-{\bf{k}})]_{\downarrow,n,\sigma}[U({\bf{p}}-{\bf{k}})]_{\downarrow,m,\sigma}\right)\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}}-{\bf{p}},m,\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-{\bf{k}},n,\downarrow}=0.

(63)

B.3
Symmetry Algebra

The eta-pairing states
\eta^{{\dagger}n}\ket{0}
are naturally interpreted as zero-momentum states of charge
+2
Cooper pairs, with
\eta^{\dagger}
being their creation operator. Because the states
\eta^{{\dagger}n}\ket{0}
are zero energy for each
n
, we can obtain eigenstates of
H
by taking superpositions with different numbers of particles. This is the simply the Cooper pair condensate that appears in the mean-field description. However, we work at fixed particle number in this work.

We now show that the eta-pairing operators form an
su(2)
charge algebra which extends the usual
u(1)
charge algebra. We compute

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\eta,\eta^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{q}\alpha\beta}[\bar{c}_{-\mathbf{k},\beta,\downarrow}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\beta,\uparrow},\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},\alpha,\downarrow}]

(64)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{q}\alpha\beta}\delta_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q}}\bar{c}_{-\mathbf{k},\beta,\downarrow}P^{\uparrow}_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},\alpha,\downarrow}-\delta_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},\alpha,\uparrow}P^{\downarrow}_{\beta\alpha}(-\mathbf{k})\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\beta,\uparrow}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\beta}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\beta,\downarrow}P^{\uparrow}_{\beta\alpha}(-\mathbf{k})\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\downarrow}-\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\uparrow}P^{\downarrow}_{\beta\alpha}(-\mathbf{k})\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\beta,\uparrow}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\beta}P^{\uparrow}_{\beta\alpha}(-\mathbf{k})P^{\downarrow}_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})-\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\downarrow}P^{\downarrow}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\beta,\downarrow}-\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\uparrow}P^{\uparrow}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\beta,\uparrow}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\beta}P^{\uparrow}_{\beta\alpha}(-\mathbf{k})P^{\uparrow}_{\alpha\beta}(-\mathbf{k})-\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\downarrow}P^{\downarrow}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\beta,\downarrow}-\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\uparrow}P^{\uparrow}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\beta,\uparrow}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\text{Tr}\ P^{\uparrow}(-\mathbf{k})\ -\bar{N}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}N_{f}-\bar{N}

where we used that
P^{\downarrow}_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=P^{\uparrow}_{\alpha\beta}(-\mathbf{k})
and
\sum_{\beta}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\beta}=\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha}
.
\bar{N}
is the total number operator so

\displaystyle[\bar{N},\eta^{\dagger}]=2\eta^{\dagger},\qquad[\bar{N},\eta]=-2\eta\ .

(65)

Alternatively, in the
\gamma
band basis,

\displaystyle[\eta,\eta^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{q},m}\sum_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime},m^{\prime}}[\gamma_{-\mathbf{q}^{\prime},m^{\prime},\downarrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{q}^{\prime},m^{\prime},\uparrow},\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},m,\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},m,\downarrow}]

(66)
