), but it is very simple to compute the spectrum numerically. The lattice model and Cooper pair band structure are depicted in Fig.
4
. The irreps of these bands are precisely those irreps induced by
s
orbitals at
3c
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\text{Cooper}}

\displaystyle=A_{1}^{3c}\uparrow 6mm=\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{5}+K_{1}\oplus K_{3}+M_{1}\oplus M_{3}\oplus M_{4}.

(160)

The Cooper pair band structure is decomposable, and as shown in Fig.
4
(b), the blue band and red bands carry irreps

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{1}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{1}+K_{1}+M_{1}

(161)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{2}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{5}+K_{3}+M_{3}\oplus M_{4}\ .

The first band carries the
\Gamma_{1}
irrep that is the zero-energy Cooper pair, and its global topology is trivial because
\Gamma_{1}+K_{1}+M_{1}
is the band representation of an
s
orbital at the 1a position. The second set of bands is fragile topological as predicted from topological quantum chemistry, and cannot induced from local orbitals.

Figure 4:
(a)
Bipartite hoppings
t
of the single-particle electron Hamiltonian Eq. (
157
) resulting in two-fold degenerate fragile flat bands. The three
s
orbitals at the 3c position are blue, and the one
s
orbital at the 1a position is marked in red.
(b)
The many-body Cooper pair/Goldstone mode spectrum
E_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})=|U|(\epsilon-\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p}))
is shown. The blue band is the quadratic gapless mode guaranteed by the
su(2)
eta-paiting symmetry. The two red bands are fragile topological (see Eq.
161
) and are gapped from the low-energy quadratic band. The the dashed black line shows the macroscopically degenerate continuum of unpaired states at
E=\epsilon|U|
.

D.5
Density Excitations

We now study the (spin) density modes which are in the form

\displaystyle\hbox{}[H,\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma^{\prime}}]\ket{GS}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m^{\prime},\sigma}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\ket{GS}[\mathcal{R}^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p})]_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime},\mathbf{k}mn}\ .

(162)

The eigenstates obtained from Eq. (
162
) are charge-neutral, so they contribute to the partition function at fixed particle-number.

To compute
\mathcal{R}^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p})
, we need the identities from App.
D.1
:

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\bar{S}^{z}_{\mathbf{q},\alpha},\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}s^{z}_{\sigma}\sum_{m}M^{nm}_{\sigma,\alpha}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q})\gamma_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q},m,\sigma}

(163)

\displaystyle\hbox{}[H,\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon|U|}{2}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma}-\frac{|U|}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{q}\alpha m}2\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}s^{z}_{\sigma}M^{nm}_{\sigma,\alpha}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q},\mathbf{q})\gamma_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q},m,\sigma}\bar{S}^{z}_{-\mathbf{q},\alpha}

and as usual, the second term in
[H,\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma}]
annihilates the groundstate. The scattering matrix
\mathcal{R}
is obtained from a direct calculation:

\displaystyle\hbox{}[H,\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma^{\prime}}]\ket{GS}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}[H,\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma^{\prime}}]\ket{GS}+[H,\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}]\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma^{\prime}}\ket{GS}

(164)

\displaystyle=\epsilon|U|\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma^{\prime}}\ket{GS}+|U|\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}\alpha m^{\prime}}s^{z}_{\sigma}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{k},m^{\prime},\sigma}M^{m^{\prime}m}_{\sigma,\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})[\bar{S}^{z}_{-\mathbf{q},\alpha},\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma^{\prime}}]\ket{GS}

\displaystyle=\epsilon|U|\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma^{\prime}}\ket{GS}

\displaystyle-|U|\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}s^{z}_{\sigma}s^{z}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\sum_{\mathbf{q}\alpha m^{\prime}n^{\prime}}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{k},\sigma,m^{\prime}}\gamma_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},\sigma^{\prime},n^{\prime}}M^{m^{\prime}m}_{\sigma,\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})M^{nn^{\prime}}_{\sigma^{\prime},\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{q},-\mathbf{q})\ket{GS}

from which we identify the scattering matrix

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\mathcal{R}^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p})]_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime},\mathbf{k}mn}

\displaystyle=\epsilon|U|\delta_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta_{nn^{\prime}}-s^{z}_{\sigma}s^{z}_{\sigma^{\prime}}|U|\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\alpha}M^{m^{\prime}m}_{\sigma,\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{p},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}-\mathbf{k})M^{nn^{\prime}}_{\sigma^{\prime},\alpha}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})

(165)

\displaystyle=\epsilon|U|\delta_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta_{nn^{\prime}}-s^{z}_{\sigma}s^{z}_{\sigma^{\prime}}|U|\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\alpha}U^{\sigma*}_{m^{\prime}\alpha}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k}^{\prime})U^{\sigma}_{m\alpha}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})U^{\sigma^{\prime}*}_{n\alpha}(\mathbf{k})U^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\alpha}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\epsilon|U|\delta_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta_{nn^{\prime}}-s^{z}_{\sigma}s^{z}_{\sigma^{\prime}}|U|\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\alpha}U^{\sigma*}_{m^{\prime}\alpha}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k}^{\prime})U^{\sigma^{\prime}}_{n^{\prime}\alpha}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\ U^{\sigma}_{m\alpha}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})U^{\sigma^{\prime}*}_{n\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\ .

Let us recall the definition from Eq. (
133
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}_{\mathbf{k}mn,\alpha}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=U^{\sigma*}_{m\alpha}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})U^{\sigma^{\prime}*}_{n\alpha}(-\mathbf{k})\ .

(166)

In the Cooper pair case (see discussion after Eq. (
133
)), the anti-commutation of the fermions, schematically
\gamma^{\dagger}_{i\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{j\uparrow}+\gamma^{\dagger}_{j\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{i\uparrow}=0
, prevented nontrivial excitations of nonzero spin. In the density excitation case,
\gamma^{\dagger}_{i\uparrow}\gamma_{i\downarrow}+\gamma^{\dagger}_{j\uparrow}\gamma_{i\uparrow}\neq 0
, so nontrivial spin
\pm 1
excitations are allowed. Enumerating the possible choices of
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
in Eq. (
162
) gives the effective Hamiltonians

\displaystyle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\uparrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\uparrow}:\qquad\mathcal{R}^{\uparrow\uparrow}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\epsilon|U|\mathbb{1}-|U|\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{U}_{\uparrow\downarrow}(\mathbf{p})\mathcal{U}_{\uparrow\downarrow}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{p})

(167)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\downarrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\downarrow}:\qquad\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow\downarrow}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\epsilon|U|\mathbb{1}-|U|\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{U}_{\downarrow\uparrow}(\mathbf{p})\mathcal{U}_{\downarrow\uparrow}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\uparrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\downarrow}:\qquad\mathcal{R}^{\uparrow\downarrow}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\epsilon|U|\mathbb{1}+|U|\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{U}_{\uparrow\uparrow}(\mathbf{p})\mathcal{U}_{\uparrow\uparrow}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\downarrow}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\uparrow}:\qquad\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow\uparrow}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\epsilon|U|\mathbb{1}+|U|\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{U}_{\downarrow\downarrow}(\mathbf{p})\mathcal{U}_{\downarrow\downarrow}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{p})\ .

Note that the first two lines are spin 0 operators and the
\mathcal{U}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}
term comes with an overall minus, while the last two lines are spin
\pm 1
and the
\mathcal{U}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}
term comes with an overall plus. Because
\mathcal{U}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}
is positive semi-definite, we learn that the spin
0
excitations are always at or below the two-electron gap
\epsilon|U|
, whereas the spin
\pm 1
excitations are at or above the
\epsilon|U|
. This leads to a simple phenomenological prediction: the spin susceptibility is exponentially suppressed at temperatures below the spin gap
\epsilon|U|
.

By the same argument in Eq. (
134
), the nonzero eigenvalues of
\mathcal{U}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}
are determined by the effective single-particle Hamiltonians

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\uparrow\uparrow}:\quad h_{\uparrow\uparrow}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=h(\mathbf{p})

(168)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow\downarrow}:\quad h_{\downarrow\downarrow}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P^{\downarrow}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P^{\uparrow}_{\alpha\beta}(-\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P^{\uparrow}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P^{\downarrow}_{\alpha\beta}(-\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=h(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\uparrow\downarrow}:\quad h_{\uparrow\downarrow}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}mn}U^{\uparrow}_{m\alpha}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})U^{\uparrow}_{n\alpha}(-\mathbf{k})U^{\uparrow*}_{m\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})U^{\uparrow*}_{n\beta}(-\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P^{\uparrow}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P^{\downarrow}_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(-\mathbf{k})^{*}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\downarrow\uparrow}:\quad h_{\downarrow\uparrow}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P^{\downarrow}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P^{\downarrow}_{\alpha\beta}(-\mathbf{k})=h_{\uparrow\downarrow}(-\mathbf{p})^{*}

so we see that the two branches of spin
0
Goldstone modes have the same spectrum as the spin 0 Cooper pair,
h(\mathbf{p})
in Eq. (
135
). This is evidence of an enlarged symmetry group, which we will explain in forthcoming work. In Eq. (
168
), the Hamiltonians
h^{\uparrow\downarrow}(\mathbf{p})
of the spin
\pm 1
density excitations are related by
\mathcal{T}
which flips the spins. If the single-particle bands have
SU(2)
spin symmetry, then
P(\mathbf{k})=P(-\mathbf{k})^{*}
and
h_{\uparrow\downarrow}(\mathbf{p})=h(\mathbf{p})
. This symmetry will manifest itself in the excitation spectrum, where there will be particle-hole symmetric excitations about the flat band continuum at
E=\epsilon|U|
. Otherwise, the spectrum of
h_{\uparrow\downarrow}(\mathbf{p})
is generically different from that of
h(\mathbf{p})
. Because our interest in this work is the low-energy excitations, we postpone a detailed study of
h_{\uparrow\downarrow}(\mathbf{p})
to future work.

We now write down the spin-0 density operators as in Eq. (
138
):

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathbf{p},\mu,\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha mn}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma}\mathcal{U}^{\sigma,-\sigma}_{\mathbf{k}mn,\alpha}(\mathbf{p})u^{\alpha}_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})

(169)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha mn}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma}U^{\sigma*}_{m\alpha}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})U^{-\sigma,*}_{n\alpha}(-\mathbf{k})u^{\alpha}_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha}\frac{u^{\alpha}_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}{\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}\alpha}\frac{u^{\alpha}_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}{\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}}e^{-i\mathbf{p}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha})}\bar{n}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}\ .

To compute the normalized states, we use Wick’s theorem. To start, we require the correlator

\displaystyle\braket{z|\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime},n^{\prime},\sigma}\gamma_{\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{k}^{\prime},m^{\prime},\sigma}\gamma^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},m,\sigma}\gamma_{\mathbf{k},n,\sigma}|z}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{\mathbf{p}\mathbf{q}}}{(1+|z|^{2})^{2}}\left(|z|^{4}\delta_{\mathbf{p},0}\delta_{n^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\delta_{nm}-0+|z|^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{nn^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\right)\braket{z|z}\ .

(170)

The presence of the
\delta_{\mathbf{p},0}
term is similar to Eq. (
140
). Following the same steps as in Eq. (
142
) and using Eq. (
141
), we find

\displaystyle\bra{z}\rho^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},\nu}\rho_{\mathbf{p},\mu}\ket{z}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\frac{(1+|z|^{2})^{N_{f}\mathcal{N}-2}}{\mathcal{N}\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\epsilon_{\nu}(\mathbf{q})}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\alpha\beta}\mathcal{U}^{\sigma,-\sigma}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}m^{\prime}n^{\prime},\beta}(\mathbf{q})^{*}u^{\beta*}_{\nu}(\mathbf{q})\left(|z|^{4}\delta_{n^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\delta_{\mathbf{p},0}\delta_{nm}+|z|^{2}\delta_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{nn^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\right)\mathcal{U}^{\sigma,-\sigma}_{\mathbf{k}mn,\alpha}(\mathbf{p})u^{\alpha}_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})

(171)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\frac{(1+|z|^{2})^{N_{f}\mathcal{N}-2}}{\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\epsilon_{\nu}(\mathbf{p})}}\sum_{\alpha\beta}u^{\beta*}_{\nu}(\mathbf{p})\left(|z|^{4}\delta_{\mathbf{p},0}\mathcal{N}\epsilon^{2}+|z|^{2}h_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{p})\right)u^{\alpha}_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\delta_{\mu,\nu}(1+|z|^{2})^{N_{f}\mathcal{N}-2}\left(|z|^{4}\delta_{\mathbf{p},0}\delta_{\mu,0}N_{f}\mathcal{N}+|z|^{2}\right)\ .

The case of
\mathbf{p}=0,\mu=0
again corresponds to the symmetry
\bar{N}_{\uparrow}
and
\bar{N}_{\downarrow}
operators at zero energy. Otherwise, we compute

\displaystyle\sum_{n}|z|^{2n}\binom{N_{f}\mathcal{N}}{n}\bra{n}\rho^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},\nu}\rho_{\mathbf{p},\mu}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}|z|^{2n+2}\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\delta_{\mu,\nu}\binom{N_{f}\mathcal{N}-2}{n},\qquad\mathbf{p}\neq 0,\mu\neq 0

(172)

\displaystyle\bra{n}\rho^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},\nu}\rho_{\mathbf{p},\mu}\ket{n}

\displaystyle=\nu(1-\nu)\delta_{\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}}\delta_{\mu,\nu}

which fixes the normalization of the states.

Appendix E
Spectrum and Topology of the Cooper Pairs

In this section, we study the effective single-particle boson Hamiltonian
h(\mathbf{p})
(Eq. (
13
) of the Main Text) in detail, first showing clearly the invariance of all many-body observables under the momentum space gauge freedom corresponding to the orbital positions (App.
E.1
). We then prove a number of generic features of the spectrum of
h(\mathbf{p})
(App.
E.2
), demonstrating a unique low-energy branch corresponding to the
s
-wave Cooper pairing channel. Its effective mass is determined by the minimal quantum metric (App.
E.3
). We then prove the symmetry properties of
h(\mathbf{p})
and determine the many-body symmetry transformations of the Cooper pair (App.
E.4
). Finally, we use topological quantum chemistry to enumerate the possible band connectivities and discover fragile topological Cooper pair bands (App.
E.5
).

E.1
Momentum Space Conventions

Before discussing the spectrum of
h(\mathbf{p})
, we discuss the dependence of
h(\mathbf{p})
on the choice of momentum space operator convention in Eq. (
16
). We start by recalling the single-particle Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\tilde{H}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\alpha\beta,\sigma}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}t^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})c_{\mathbf{R}^{\prime},\beta,\sigma}=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\beta,\sigma}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}\tilde{h}^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k},\beta,\sigma}

(173)

where the momentum operators are
c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha})}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\sigma}
. The choice of the
\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}
term in the momentum space operators is arbitrary but convenient for representations of the space group symmetries
[
109
,
19
]
. We now show that the spectrum of
h(\mathbf{p})
and the many-body operators
\eta^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p},\mu}
are all invariant under
\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}\to\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}+\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
. However, under
\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}\to\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}+\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
, we see nontrivial transformations

\displaystyle c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}

\displaystyle\to e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\sigma}

(174)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle\to e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}}\tilde{h}^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\beta}}\ .

If we define the local embedding matrix
[V_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{k})]_{\alpha\beta}=e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}}\delta_{\alpha\beta}
, then the transformation can be written
\tilde{h}(\mathbf{k})\to V_{\mathbf{x}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})\tilde{h}(\mathbf{k})V_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{k})
. Thus the electron eigenvectors
U_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})
obey
U_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})\to V_{\mathbf{x}}^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})U_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})
and the projectors transform like
\tilde{h}
:
P_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})\to V^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{k})P_{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})V_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{k})
. As such, we have

\displaystyle P^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})\to e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{x}_{\beta})}P^{\sigma}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})\ .

(175)

We now consider the transformation of the Cooper pair Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\to\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}e^{i(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})\cdot(\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{x}_{\beta})}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{x}_{\beta}-\mathbf{x}_{\alpha})}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})

(176)

\displaystyle=e^{i\mathbf{p}\cdot(\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{x}_{\beta})}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=[V^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{p})h(\mathbf{p})V_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{p})]_{\alpha\beta}\ .

Hence the Cooper pair eigenvectors transform just like the electron eigenvectors:
u_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\to V^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{p})u_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})
. We see that the Cooper pair operators are invariant under the embedding:

\displaystyle\eta^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p},\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha}\frac{u^{\alpha}_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}{\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},\alpha,\downarrow}

(177)

\displaystyle\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha}\frac{u^{\alpha}_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})e^{i\mathbf{p}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}}}{\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{p})\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},\alpha,\uparrow}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},\alpha,\downarrow}

\displaystyle=\eta^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p},\mu}

It is also obvious that the spectrum of the Cooper pairs,
\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})
, is invariant because the
V_{\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{k})
conjugate the Cooper pair Hamiltonian. Hence, as expected, all of the many-body observables are invariant under the arbitrary choice of
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
.

E.2
Cooper Pair Hamiltonian and Spectrum

The fact that the Cooper pair and density excitations are determined by an effective single-particle Hamiltonian
h(\mathbf{p})
is very evocative. It shows that the tightly-bound Cooper pairs behave much like a free boson whose hoppings arise from the quantum geometry, encoded in
P(\mathbf{k})
, of the flat bands. We will study the effective single-particle boson Hamiltonian and prove a number of properties about its spectrum and topology.

Let us restate the effective Hamiltonian derived in Eq. (
135
),

\displaystyle h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})

(178)

where
P(\mathbf{k})=U(\mathbf{k})U^{\dagger}(\mathbf{k})
is the Hermitian projector into the spin-
\uparrow
electronic flat bands. The
\alpha,\beta
orbital indices range from
1,\dots,N_{orb}
. In the bipartite crystalline lattice construction,
N_{orb}\to N_{L}
is understood to be the number of orbitals in the
L
sublattice. (
P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})=0
for
\alpha
or
\beta\notin L
.)

First we show
h(\mathbf{p})
is Hermitian:

\displaystyle h^{*}_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{p})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P^{*}_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P^{*}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})\ .

(179)

The eigenvalues of
h(\mathbf{p})
are denoted by
\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})
where
\mu=0,\dots,N_{orb}-1
will denote the boson band index. We will now show that the spectrum of
h(\mathbf{p})
is bounded by
0\leq\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\leq\epsilon
. Importantly, the many-body energies (see Eq. (
134
)) of the boson excitations are given by
|U|(\epsilon-\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p}))
so the bounds on the eigenvalues of
h(\mathbf{p})
show that the many-body spectrum of the boson bands is between
0
and
\epsilon|U|
. Note that the largest eigenvalue of
h(\mathbf{p})
corresponds to the lowest energy in the many-body spectrum. Thus we can think of
|U|\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})
as the binding energy gained by the pairing relative to the unpaired continuum of states at energy
\epsilon|U|
.

To show
h(\mathbf{p})
is positive semi-definite, we observe that for an arbitrary vector
v
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\beta}v^{*}_{\alpha}h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})v_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{mn,\alpha\beta}v^{*}_{\alpha}U_{\alpha m}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})U^{*}_{\beta m}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})U_{\beta n}(\mathbf{k})U^{*}_{\alpha n}(\mathbf{k})v_{\beta}

(180)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{mn}[\sum_{\alpha}U^{*}_{\alpha m}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})v_{\alpha}U_{\alpha n}(\mathbf{k})]^{*}\sum_{\beta}U^{*}_{\beta m}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})v_{\beta}U_{\beta n}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{mn}V^{*}_{mn}V_{mn}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}||V||_{F}^{2}\geq 0\ .

where we defined the
N_{f}\times N_{f}
matrix
V_{mn}=\sum_{\beta}U^{*}_{\beta m}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})v_{\beta}U_{\beta n}(\mathbf{k})
and used the Frobenius norm
||V||_{F}^{2}=\sum_{mn}|V_{mn}|^{2}
. This establishes
\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\geq 0
. Alternatively, we observe that
P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=[P(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})\circ P^{T}(\mathbf{k})]_{\alpha\beta}
is the Hadamard product of two positive semi-definite matrices
P(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})
and
P^{T}(\mathbf{k})
. By the Schur product theorem,
P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})
is positive semi-definite, and so
h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})
is the sum of positive semi-definite matrices with positive coefficients, and hence is positive-semi definite itself.

To prove
\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\leq\epsilon
, we show that
\epsilon\mathbb{1}-h(\mathbf{p})
is positive semi-definite. Using the uniform pairing condition, note that

\displaystyle\hbox{}[\epsilon\mathbb{1}-h(\mathbf{p})]_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\epsilon\delta_{\alpha\beta}\,-h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\delta_{\beta\alpha}\ -h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})

(181)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})\delta_{\beta\alpha}\ -h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})(\delta_{\beta\alpha}-P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})Q_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})

where we defined
Q(\mathbf{k})=\mathbb{1}-P(\mathbf{k})
. Note that
Q(\mathbf{k})
is also a positive semi-definite matrix (it is a projector), and so
\epsilon\mathbb{1}-h(\mathbf{p})
is positive semi-definite by the Schur product theorem.

We have shown that
\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\leq\epsilon
. We now prove that this bound is tight by exhibiting the corresponding eigenvector at
\mathbf{p}=0
. Claim: there is an eigenvector
u_{0}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{p}=0)=1/\sqrt{N_{orb}}
with eigenvalue
\epsilon
. This is simply the normalized uniform eigenvector corresponding to the eta-pairing symmetry
\eta^{\dagger}
. The claim is proven using the uniform pairing condition:

\displaystyle\hbox{}[h(0)u_{0}]_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{\beta}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})u_{0}^{\beta}(0)

(182)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{orb}}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{\beta}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{orb}}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}[P(\mathbf{k})^{2}]_{\alpha\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{orb}}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\epsilon u^{\alpha}_{0}\ .

The existence of this state is tied to the
\eta^{\dagger}
symmetry. The
\mathbf{p}=0
Cooper pair excitation spectrum includes the
\eta^{\dagger}
operator in its Hilbert space, and
\eta^{\dagger}
commutes with the Hamiltonian and therefore has zero energy. Because the many-body energy is
|U|(\epsilon-\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p}))
, there must be a band with
\epsilon_{\mu}(0)=\epsilon
, and as such this excitation is gapless. We refer to this as the
\mu=0
band. Indeed, the excitation operator defined in Eq. (
138
) yields

\displaystyle\eta^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p}=0,\mu=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha}\frac{1/\sqrt{N_{orb}}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},\alpha,\downarrow}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{f}\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},\alpha,\downarrow}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{f}\mathcal{N}}}\eta^{\dagger}

(183)

using
\epsilon=N_{f}/N_{orb}
. (In the bipartite case,
N_{orb}\to N_{L}
.)

We now show that the gapless Cooper pair band is unique, i.e. that there are no other zero-energy excitations for all
\mathbf{p}
. To do so, we first Fourier transform into real space using Eq. (
23
), which reads

\displaystyle P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})=\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{r}_{\beta})}p_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R})\ .

(184)

A direct calculation gives

\displaystyle h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}e^{i(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{r}_{\beta})+i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{R}^{\prime}+\mathbf{r}_{\beta}-\mathbf{r}_{\alpha})}p_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R})p_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})

(185)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{i\mathbf{p}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{r}_{\beta})}p_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R})p_{\beta\alpha}(-\mathbf{R})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{i\mathbf{p}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{r}_{\beta})}|p_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R})|^{2}

using
p^{\dagger}(\mathbf{R})=p(-\mathbf{R})
. We recognize the last line of Eq. (
185
) as an expression for the matrix elements of the real space hopping matrix
t_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})=|p_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})|^{2}
. This shows that the effective hoppings felt by the bosons are due to the correlation function
p(\mathbf{R})
of the flat bands. Note that
\text{spec }t_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})=\text{spec }|p_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})|^{2}=\{\text{spec }h(\mathbf{p})\forall\mathbf{p}\}
. To show that
\epsilon_{0}(\mathbf{p}=0)
is the unique largest eigenvalue of the whole band structure of
h(\mathbf{p})
, the crucial feature is that the hopping elements are non-negative:
|p_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})|^{2}\geq 0
. Hence we can apply the Perron-Frobenius theorem, which states than a non-negative irreducible matrix has a
unique
largest positive eigenvalue, which we have shown explicitly is
\epsilon
. Because
|p_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})|^{2}
is non-negative, we must also check that
t_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
is irreducible to apply the theorem. An irreducible matrix
A
is one for which
Ae\notin e\forall e
where
e
is a basis of any coordinate subspace. Physically, irreducibility is equivalent to the lattice being strongly connected, meaning any two orbitals are connected by a sequence of hoppings
|p_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})|^{2}
. This is
not
the case in a trivial atomic limit where
p_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})\propto\delta_{\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}^{\prime}}
. Ref.
[
19
]
proved nonzero lower bounds on
||p(\mathbf{R}\neq 0)||^{2}
in terms of real space invariants
[
33
]
which are the indices diagnosing obstructed atomic insulators and fragile phases. Excluding certain pathological cases (like a 2D system formed from decoupled 1D chains, or an occupied trivial atomic limit band added with a direct sum into the projector
P(\mathbf{k})
), the results of Ref.
[
19
]
show that
t_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{\prime})
is strongly connected if the flat bands are in an obstructed, fragile, or stable phase.

We prove a further result on the spectrum of
h(\mathbf{p})
. So far we have shown that
0\leq\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\leq\epsilon
. We now show that the spectral average of the band structure is
\epsilon^{2}
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}N_{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\mu}\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}N_{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}}\text{Tr}\ h(\mathbf{p})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}N_{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\alpha}h_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{p})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}^{2}N_{L}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\mathbf{k}\alpha}P_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{N_{L}}\sum_{\alpha}\epsilon^{2}=\epsilon^{2}\ .

(186)

Recalling the many-body energies
E_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})=|U|(\epsilon-\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p}))
, the many-body density of states
g(E)
obeys

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\epsilon|U|}Eg(E)dE=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\mu}E_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})=|U|N_{L}(\epsilon-\epsilon^{2})=|U|N_{f}(1-\epsilon)\ .

(187)

The upper bound of integral in Eq. (
187
) is understood to be just below
\epsilon|U|
, where there is a thermodynamically large degeneracy of flat density excitation bands (analogous to the unpaired continuum in the Cooper pair sector).

Our last result concerns a simple transformation of the model under
P(\mathbf{k})\to\mathbb{1}-P(\mathbf{k})
, which is equivalent to exchanging the projected and unprojected bands. For simplicity, we assume that all orbitals form an irrep of a single Wyckoff position, e.g. we do not use the bipartite construction in what follows. Note that under
P(\mathbf{k})\to\mathbb{1}-P(\mathbf{k})
, we have
N_{f}\to N_{orb}-N_{f}
and hence
\epsilon\to 1-\epsilon
. Now we compute

\displaystyle h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\to\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}(\delta_{\alpha\beta}-P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{p}))(\delta_{\alpha\beta}-P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k}))

(188)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha\beta}-\delta_{\alpha\beta}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{p})-\delta_{\alpha\beta}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\alpha}(\mathbf{k})+h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha\beta}-2\epsilon\delta_{\alpha\beta}+h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})\ .

Thus the many-body energies transform as

\displaystyle E_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})=|U|(\epsilon-\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p}))\to|U|\big(1-\epsilon-(1-2\epsilon+\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p}))\big)=|U|(\epsilon-\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p}))

(189)

and hence are invariant. The energy of the unpaired continuum states does change, however,
\epsilon|U|\to(1-\epsilon)|U|
. Because we proved above (Eq. (
181
)) that the bound state energies
E_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\leq\epsilon|U|
are below the unpaired continuum, we see in fact that
E_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\leq\min\{\epsilon|U|,(1-\epsilon)|U|\}
. This follows by taking
P(\mathbf{k})\to\mathbb{1}-P(\mathbf{k})
and using
E_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\to E_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})
,
\epsilon\to 1-\epsilon
.

E.3
Cooper Pair mass and Minimal Quantum Metric

We proved in App.
E.2
that the many-body spectrum contains a gapless mode
E_{\mu=0}(\mathbf{p}=0)=0
. In this section, we use perturbation theory to compute the small
\mathbf{p}
expansion around this point, yielding the low energy behavior of the bosonic excitations. The result we will prove is
\epsilon_{0}(\mathbf{p})=\epsilon-\frac{g_{ij}}{N_{L}}p_{i}p_{j}
where
g_{ij}
is the minimal quantum metric (introduced in Ref.
[
61
]
and explicitly constructed below) and
N_{L}
is the number of orbitals where
P(\mathbf{k})
is nonzero. We sum over repeated spatial indices
i,j
in this section. As a result, the many-body energy is

\displaystyle E_{0}(\mathbf{p})=|U|(\epsilon-\epsilon_{0}(\mathbf{p}))=|U|\frac{g_{ij}}{N_{L}}p_{i}p_{j}+\dots{}

(190)

which shows the low-lying Goldstone and Cooper pair modes are quadratic. This amounts to a many-body calculation of the Cooper pair mass, which has been previously approximated from the two-body problem
[
4
]
and is directly related to the mean-field superfluid weight
[
15
,
1
]
.

Because we have proven that
\epsilon_{0}(\mathbf{p}=0)
is the unique maximal eigenvalue in App.
E.2
, its small
\mathbf{p}
corrections can be obtained using non-degenerate perturbation theory. At zeroth order,
h(0)
has eigenvectors
u_{\mu}
, with
u_{0}=1/\sqrt{N_{L}}
corresponding to the maximal eigenvalue
\epsilon_{\mu}=\epsilon
. We determine the corrections to
h(0)
by expanding in
\mathbf{p}
:

\displaystyle h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})+p_{i}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\partial_{i}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})+\frac{1}{2}p_{i}p_{j}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\partial_{ij}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})+\dots\ .

(191)

Denote
h^{i}_{\alpha\beta}=\partial_{i}h_{\alpha\beta}|_{\mathbf{p}=0}
. By time-reversal
h^{*}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})=h_{\alpha\beta}(-\mathbf{p})
, we expand to first order and find

\displaystyle(p_{i}h^{i}_{\alpha\beta})^{*}

\displaystyle=-p_{i}h^{i}_{\alpha\beta},

(192)

so
h^{i}_{\alpha\beta}=-h^{i}_{\beta\alpha}
is anti-symmetric because
(h^{i}_{\alpha\beta})^{*}=h^{i}_{\beta\alpha}
using Hermiticity. This can also be seen with an integration by parts. This immediately shows that the first order correction to
\epsilon_{0}(\mathbf{p})
vanishes because

\displaystyle u^{\dagger}_{0}h^{i}u_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{L}}\sum_{\alpha\beta}h^{i}_{\alpha\beta}=0\ .

(193)

We now consider the second order correction. There are contributions to the second order correction:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}p_{i}p_{j}\partial_{ij}\epsilon_{0}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}p_{i}p_{j}\left(2\Re\sum_{\mu\neq 0}\frac{u^{\dagger}_{0}h^{i}u_{\mu}u_{\mu}^{\dagger}h^{j}u_{0}}{\epsilon-\epsilon_{\mu}(0)}+\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\beta}\frac{1}{N_{L}}\partial_{ij}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})\right)\equiv\frac{1}{2}p_{i}p_{j}(A_{ij}+B_{ij})\ .

(194)

The first term
A_{ij}
is the usual second order correction from a linear perturbation
p_{i}h^{i}
, and the second term
B_{ij}
is simply the expectation value of the second order correction in
u_{0}
. The second term is related to the quantum metric after an integration by parts:

\displaystyle B_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{L}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\beta}\partial_{ij}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=-\frac{1}{N_{L}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\text{Tr}\ \partial_{i}P(\mathbf{k})\partial_{j}P(\mathbf{k})=-\frac{2}{N_{L}}g_{ij}\preceq 0,

(195)

\displaystyle g_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}g_{ij}(\mathbf{k}),\qquad g_{ij}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}\ \partial_{i}P(\mathbf{k})\partial_{j}P(\mathbf{k})

using the convention
\text{Tr}\ \mathcal{G}_{ij}(\mathbf{k})=\text{Tr}\ P(\mathbf{k})\partial_{i}P(\mathbf{k})\partial_{j}P(\mathbf{k})=g_{ij}(\mathbf{k})-\frac{i}{2}f_{ij}(\mathbf{k})
. Note that Ref.
[
19
]
proves
\text{Tr}\ P(\mathbf{k})\{\partial_{i}P(\mathbf{k}),\partial_{j}P(\mathbf{k})\}=\text{Tr}\ \partial_{i}P(\mathbf{k})\partial_{j}P(\mathbf{k})
. Next, we observe that
A_{ij}\succeq 0
because
\epsilon-\epsilon_{\mu}(0)\geq 0
. Thus
A
and and
B
have competing contributions to
\partial_{ij}\epsilon_{0}(\mathbf{p})
.

Because the spectrum of
h(\mathbf{p})
is invariant under invariant under
\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}\to\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}+\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
,
\frac{1}{2}p_{i}p_{j}\partial_{ij}\epsilon_{0}(\mathbf{p})
is invariant. However,
A_{ij}
and
B_{ij}
are not individually invariant. We now show that there is a unique choice of
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
, up to a constant, where
A_{ij}=0
and the quantum metric alone determines
\partial_{ij}\epsilon_{0}(\mathbf{p})
. Because
A_{ij}+B_{ij}
is independent of
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
, taking
A_{ij}\to 0
corresponds to the maximal
B_{ij}
and hence the minimal
g_{ij}
for all
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
.

First we show that there exists
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
where
A_{ij}=0
, and then we show such a choice respects the symmetries of the system. Note that we keep
\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}
, the physical location of the Wyckoff position, constant.

Returning to Eq. (
194
), we recall that
\epsilon-\epsilon_{\mu}>0
and
u^{\dagger}_{0}p_{i}h^{i}u_{\mu}u_{\mu}^{\dagger}p_{j}h^{j}u_{0}
is nonnegative. Thus
A_{ij}=0
iff
u_{\mu}^{\dagger}h^{j}u_{0}=0
for all
\mu\neq 0
. Because the
u_{\mu}
eigenvectors form a complete and orthogonal basis,
u_{\mu}^{\dagger}h^{j}u_{0}=0
iff
h^{j}u_{0}\propto u_{0}
. Thus
u_{0}
must be an eigenvector of
h^{i}
. For a given choice of orbital locations, this is not guaranteed. However,
h^{i}
is not invariant under shifts of orbital location
\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}\to\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}+\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
. Using
P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})\to e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}-\mathbf{x}_{\beta})}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})
, the transformation is

\displaystyle h^{i}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\partial_{i}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle\to\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\partial_{i}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})+i(x^{i}_{\alpha}-x^{i}_{\beta})\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})

(196)

\displaystyle=h^{i}_{\alpha\beta}+i(x^{i}_{\alpha}-x^{i}_{\beta})h_{\alpha\beta}(0)

and
x^{i}_{\alpha}
denotes the
i
th spatial component of
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
. Note that
h_{\alpha\beta}(0)
is Hermitian and real, so
i(x^{i}_{\alpha}-x^{i}_{\beta})h_{\alpha\beta}(0)
is Hermitian and anti-symmetric, as is
h^{i}_{\alpha\beta}
. If
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}=\mathbf{x}
is a constant shift, then
h^{i}_{\alpha\beta}\to h^{i}_{\alpha\beta}
.

Our task is to look for
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
such that
u_{0}
is an eigenvector of
h^{i}
(which guarantees that
A_{ij}=0
). The eigenvalue equation is

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta}(h^{i}_{\alpha\beta}+i(x^{i}_{\alpha}-x^{i}_{\beta})h_{\alpha\beta}(0))u^{\beta}_{0}=\lambda u^{\alpha}_{0}

(197)

where
\lambda
is the eigenvalue. First we show that if Eq. (
197
) condition holds, then
\lambda=0
. Contracting both sides with
u^{\alpha}_{0}=1/\sqrt{N_{L}}
gives

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{L}}\sum_{\alpha\beta}h^{i}_{\alpha\beta}+i(x^{i}_{\alpha}-x^{i}_{\beta})h_{\alpha\beta}(0)=0

(198)

because of anti-symmetry in
\alpha\beta
. Then Eq. (
197
) reads

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}h^{j}_{\alpha\beta}+i\sum_{\beta}(x^{j}_{\alpha}-x^{j}_{\beta})h_{\alpha\beta}(0)

(199)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}h^{j}_{\alpha\beta}+ix^{j}_{\alpha}\sum_{\beta}h_{\alpha\beta}(0)-i\sum_{\beta}h_{\alpha\beta}(0)x^{j}_{\beta}

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta}h_{\alpha\beta}(0)x^{j}_{\beta}

\displaystyle=-i\sum_{\beta}h^{j}_{\alpha\beta}+\epsilon x^{j}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\hbox{}[(h(0)-\epsilon)x^{j}]_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-i\sum_{\beta}h^{j}_{\alpha\beta}

where we used the uniform pairing condition
\sum_{\beta}h_{\alpha\beta}(0)=\epsilon
. We now define the vector
-i\sum_{\beta}h^{j}_{\alpha\beta}=h^{j}_{\alpha}
which is real and obeys
\sum_{\alpha}h^{j}_{\alpha}=0
, so it is orthogonal to the uniform eigenvector
u_{0}
. Because
h(0)
has a unique eigenvector
u_{0}
with eigenvalue
\epsilon
(see Eq. (
182
)),
(h(0)-\epsilon)
is invertible up to the
u_{0}
eigenspace. This means
x^{j}_{\alpha}
is uniquely determined up to constant shifts because
u_{0}^{\alpha}\propto 1
. Thus we obtain
x^{j}_{\alpha}
up to constant shifts by applying the pseudo-inverse
[
77
]

(h(0)-\epsilon)^{+}
:

\displaystyle x^{j}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=[-i(h(0)-\epsilon)^{+}h^{j}u_{0}\sqrt{N_{L}}]_{\alpha},\qquad[h^{j}u_{0}\sqrt{N_{L}}]_{\alpha}=\sum_{\beta}h^{j}_{\alpha\beta}

(200)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=[i(\epsilon-h(0))^{+}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}h(\mathbf{p})|_{\mathbf{p}=0})u_{0}\sqrt{N_{L}}]_{\alpha}\ .

The pseudo-inverse of a Hermitian matrix is very simple. For a hermitian matrix
h
with eigenvalues
\epsilon_{\mu}
and eigen-decomposition
h=\sum_{\mu}\epsilon_{\mu}p_{\mu}
, the pseudo-inverse is
h^{+}=\sum_{\mu}\epsilon^{+}_{\mu}p_{\mu}
where
p_{\mu}
is the projector onto the
\epsilon_{\mu}
eigenspace,
\epsilon^{+}=1/\epsilon
if
\epsilon\neq 0
, and
\epsilon^{+}=0
otherwise. Hence
hh^{+}=h^{+}h
acts as the identity on the nonzero eigenspaces and 0 otherwise.

Having proven in Eq. (
200
) that orbitals shifts
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
always exist for which
A_{ij}=0
, we now show that
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
preserve the space group symmetries
g\in G
. In particular, this will show that orbitals at high-symmetry Wyckoff positions, whose location is fixed by symmetry, are necessarily the choices where
A_{ij}=0
. In this case, the minimal quantum metric is the quantum metric evaluated in the
\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}
convention Eq. (
16
).

To prove this result, we need the action of the space group symmetries on
h(\mathbf{p})
. We prove in App.
E.4
that

\displaystyle\Gamma[g]h(\mathbf{p})\Gamma^{\dagger}[g]=h(g^{-1}\mathbf{p}),\qquad g\in G

(201)

where the representation
\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}[g]=|D_{\alpha\beta}[g]|
is a permutation matrix. It follows that

\displaystyle\Gamma[g]\boldsymbol{\nabla}h(\mathbf{p})\Gamma^{\dagger}[g]=\boldsymbol{\nabla}h(g^{-1}\mathbf{p})=g^{-1}\boldsymbol{\nabla}h|_{g^{-1}\mathbf{p}}\ .

(202)

Evaluating Eq. (
202
) at
\mathbf{p}=0
gives
\Gamma[g]\boldsymbol{\nabla}h|_{\mathbf{p}=0}\Gamma^{\dagger}[g]=g^{-1}\boldsymbol{\nabla}h|_{\mathbf{p}=0}
. Then

\displaystyle g^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=[i(\epsilon-h(0))^{+}(g^{-1}\boldsymbol{\nabla}h(\mathbf{p})|_{\mathbf{p}=0})u_{0}]_{\alpha}

(203)

\displaystyle=[i(\epsilon-h(0))^{+}\Gamma[g](\boldsymbol{\nabla}h(\mathbf{p})|_{\mathbf{p}=0})\Gamma^{\dagger}[g]u_{0}]_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}[g][i(\epsilon-h(0))^{+}(\boldsymbol{\nabla}h(\mathbf{p})|_{\mathbf{p}=0})[g]u_{0}]_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}[g]\mathbf{x}_{\beta}

where we used
\Gamma[g]h(0)\Gamma^{\dagger}[g]=h(0)
and
\Gamma[g]u_{0}=u_{0}
because
\Gamma[g]
is a permutation matrix and
u_{0}
is the uniform eigenvector. Thus if two orbitals are related by a symmetry
g
, their
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
that guarantee the minimal metric are also related by
g
. With Eq. (
203
), we can prove that
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}=0
if the site symmetry group
G_{x}
of the Wyckoff position
x
contains a symmetry
h
such that
h\mathbf{v}\neq\mathbf{v}
for all
\mathbf{v}\neq 0
and
\Gamma[h]=\mathbb{1}
. As an example,
h
can be any nontrivial rotation at a maximal Wyckoff position. In this case

\displaystyle h\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}=\sum_{\beta}\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}[h]\mathbf{x}_{\beta}=\mathbf{x}_{\alpha},

(204)

but by assumption, the only solution to
h\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}=\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
is
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}=0
. We give two examples in space group
p4mm
(see Fig.
5
). (1) Consider an
s
(or
p
) orbital at the 2c
=\{\frac{1}{2}\hat{x},\frac{1}{2}\hat{y}\}
position. The site symmetry group is
G_{2c}=2mm
consisting of
C_{2}
reflection and mirrors. The
s
orbitals transform in a 1D irrep, so
\Gamma[C_{2}]=1
. Thus
-\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}=C_{2}\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}=\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
, so
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}=0
. (2) Consider
p_{x},p_{y}
orbitals at the 1b
=\frac{1}{2}\hat{x}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{y}
position. The representation of
C_{4}
is
D[C_{4}]=i\sigma_{2}
(the
p_{x},p_{y}
orbitals are rotated into each other), so
\Gamma[C_{4}]=\sigma_{1}
. Then
\Gamma[C_{2}]=\mathbb{1}
and
-\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}=C_{2}\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}=\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
as before. We remark that if the orbitals are not at a maximal Wyckoff position (such as the 6j position in App.
F
), the symmetries allow
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}
to be nonzero.

Figure 5:
Unit cell of the wallpaper group
p4mm
generated by
C_{4}
and translations (we take
\mathbf{a}_{1}=(1,0),\mathbf{a}_{2}=(0,1)
for ease). There are three maximal Wyckoff positons 1a =
(0,0)
,1b=
(1/2,1/2)
,2c=
\{(0,1/2),(1/2,0)\}
. The site-symmetry groups are
G_{1a}=4mm,G_{1b}=4mm,G_{2c}=2mm
. In all cases, the rotations of the site-symmetry groups are enough to pin
\mathbf{x}_{\alpha}=0
.

E.4
Space Group Symmetry and Representations of the Boson Bands

Because
h(\mathbf{p})
is a single-particle Hamiltonian, its bands can display nontrivial topology. In fact, we will show that topological quantum chemistry can be applied to
h(\mathbf{p})
to prove a number of nontrivial statements about the possible band decompositions and symmetry representations. To bring this formalism to bear, we must first work out the symmetries of
h(\mathbf{p})
. We denote the
N_{L}\times N_{L}
representations of the symmetries of
h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})
by
\Gamma[g]
.

First we show that
h(\mathbf{p})
has a bosonic time-reversal symmetry with representation
\Gamma[\mathcal{T}]=K
. We check

\displaystyle h^{*}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P^{*}_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P^{*}_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=h_{\alpha\beta}(-\mathbf{p})\ .

(205)

We also need to check the embedding matrices. Using
P(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G})=V[\mathbf{G}]P(\mathbf{k})V^{\dagger}[\mathbf{G}]
where
V_{\alpha\beta}[\mathbf{G}]=\delta_{\alpha\beta}e^{-i\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}\cdot\mathbf{G}}
is the embedding matrix and
\mathbf{G}
is a reciprocal lattice vector (see the Appendix of Ref.
[
19
]
), we compute

\displaystyle h_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{G})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{G}+\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}e^{-i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}}P_{\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})e^{i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}_{\beta}}P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=[V(\mathbf{G})h(\mathbf{p})V^{\dagger}(\mathbf{G})]_{\alpha\beta}

(206)

which is the same as the transformation of the electron Hamiltonian. The embedding matrix contains the location
\mathbf{r}_{\alpha}
of the orbital basis in the lattice. Since the embedding matrix
V[\mathbf{G}]
of
h(\mathbf{p})
is the same as the initial electron Hamiltonian (see Eq. (
22
)), we find that the orbitals of the effective boson tight-binding model are located at the same position as the electron orbitals. The representations of the orbitals, however, may be different.

To study the spatial symmetries, we recall that
D[g]P(\mathbf{k})D^{\dagger}[g]=P(g\mathbf{k})
(see Eq. (
32
)). In indices, we have

\displaystyle h_{\alpha\beta}(g\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(g\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}P_{\alpha\beta}(g\mathbf{p}+g\mathbf{k})P_{\beta\alpha}(g\mathbf{k})

(207)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\kappa\kappa^{\prime}\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}D_{\alpha\kappa}[g]P_{\kappa\kappa^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})D_{\beta\kappa^{\prime}}^{*}[g]D_{\beta\lambda}[g]P_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})D_{\alpha\lambda^{\prime}}^{*}[g]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\kappa\kappa^{\prime}\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}D_{\alpha\kappa}[g]D_{\alpha\lambda^{\prime}}^{*}[g]\ P_{\kappa\kappa^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})\ D_{\beta\kappa^{\prime}}^{*}[g]D_{\beta\lambda}[g].

We now need the following fact which we prove momentarily: in every point group, there exists a choice of basis where

\displaystyle D_{\alpha\kappa}[g]D_{\alpha\lambda^{\prime}}^{*}[g]=\delta_{\lambda\kappa}|D_{\alpha\lambda}[g]|^{2},\qquad g\in G\ .

(208)

To understand Eq. (
208
), we see that each column of
D[g]
can only have a single non-zero element. Because
D[g]
is unitary, this element must be a complex phase. Thus Eq. (
208
) is equivalent to the statement that
D[g]
is a complex permutation matrix. This is trivially satisfied if there is a single orbital per unit cell. More generally, a group with this property, that there exists a basis where all irreps are complex permutation matrices, is called a monomial group. It was recently proven that all spinless point groups in 2D and 3D are monomial
[
85
]
. Because
D[g]
are spinless representations of the point group (or little group) of the
\Gamma
point
\mathbf{p}=0
, Eq. (
208
) holds. Conveniently, all the representation matrices given on the Bilbao crystallographic server
https://www.cryst.ehu.es/cgi-bin/cryst/programs/representations_point.pl?tipogrupo=spg
are in the complex permutation matrix form so there is only one nonzero entry per row. However, recall that our convention in App.
A.2.1
is to take
D[\mathcal{T}]
to act as the identity on the orbital index. It is now convenient to define

\displaystyle D_{\alpha\kappa}[g]D_{\alpha\lambda^{\prime}}^{*}[g]=\delta_{\lambda\kappa}|D_{\alpha\lambda}[g]|^{2}=\delta_{\lambda\kappa}|D_{\alpha\lambda}[g]|\equiv\delta_{\lambda\kappa}\Gamma_{\alpha\lambda}[g],\qquad\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}[g]=|D_{\alpha\beta}[g]|

(209)

where
\Gamma[g]
, which are real permutation matrices, are the representations of
g\in G
on
h(\mathbf{p})
. This follows from Eqs. (
207
) and (
209
):

\displaystyle h_{\alpha\beta}(g\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}D_{\alpha\lambda}[g]D_{\alpha\lambda}^{*}[g]P_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\mathbf{k})D_{\beta\lambda^{\prime}}^{*}[g]D_{\beta\lambda^{\prime}}[g]

(210)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\Gamma_{\alpha\lambda}[g]P_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k})P_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\mathbf{k})\Gamma_{\beta\lambda^{\prime}}[g]

\displaystyle=[\,\Gamma[g]h(\mathbf{p})\Gamma^{\dagger}[g]\,]_{\alpha\beta}.

In this way, we have shown that
h(\mathbf{p})
possess all the space group symmetries of the electron Hamiltonian, but in a “bosonized” form. Because
\Gamma[g]
are all real permutation matrices, we can think of the boson orbitals as those obtained from replacing all electron orbitals with
s
orbitals. We emphasize that this result holds on the UPC lattices we have constructed, where the orbitals form an irrep of a single Wyckoff position (App.
A.2
).

Because
\Gamma[g]
is real, it is obvious that
\Gamma[\mathcal{T}]
commutes with all spatial operators, so the topology of the Cooper pair bound states is classified by time-reversal symmetric space groups with
\Gamma[\mathcal{T}]^{2}=+1
. There is an additional constraint on the spectrum: we proved that
u_{0}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{p}=0)=1/\sqrt{N_{L}}
. But
\Gamma[g]
is a permutation matrix, so
\Gamma[g]u_{0}(\mathbf{p}=0)=u_{0}(\mathbf{p}=0)
and hence the irrep of the
\epsilon_{0}(\mathbf{p})
band at
\mathbf{p}=0
is always the trivial irrep. We now show that in the higher Cooper pair bands and at nonzero momenta, the Cooper pair wavefunction can (and will) exhibit other pairing symmetries.

Using the representation matrices
\Gamma[g]
, we can compute the action of
g
on the many-body Cooper pair creation operators. From Eq. (
138
), we first derive

\displaystyle g\eta^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p},\mu}g^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\frac{u^{\alpha}_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}{\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{g\mathbf{p}+g\mathbf{k},\alpha^{\prime},\uparrow}D^{\uparrow}_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[g]\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-g\mathbf{k},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\downarrow}D^{\downarrow}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}\alpha}[g]

(211)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{g\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},\alpha^{\prime},\uparrow}D_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[g]D^{*}_{\alpha^{\prime\prime}\alpha}[g]u^{\alpha}_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\downarrow}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mu}(g\mathbf{p})}}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{g\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},\alpha^{\prime},\uparrow}\delta_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\Gamma_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[g]u^{\alpha}_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},\alpha^{\prime\prime},\downarrow}

where we used that
D[g]=D^{\uparrow}[g]=D^{\downarrow}[g]^{*}
by
\mathcal{T}
and
S_{z}
. Now we use the space group symmetries of
h(\mathbf{p})
such that
\epsilon_{\mu}(g\mathbf{p})=\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})
and
\sum_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}[g]u^{\alpha}_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})=\sum_{\mu^{\prime}}u^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{\mu^{\prime}}(g\mathbf{p})B_{\mu^{\prime}\mu}^{g}(\mathbf{p})
where
[B^{g}(\mathbf{p})]_{\mu^{\prime}\mu}=u^{\dagger}_{\mu^{\prime}}(g\mathbf{p})\Gamma[g]u_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})
is the sewing matrix of the bands
[
25
]
. If
\epsilon_{\mu}(\mathbf{p})\neq\epsilon_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbf{p})
, then
B^{g}_{\mu^{\prime}\mu}(\mathbf{p})=0
and thus

\displaystyle g\eta^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{p},\mu}g^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{N}}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\alpha\mu^{\prime}}\frac{u^{\alpha}_{\mu^{\prime}}(g\mathbf{p})}{\sqrt{\epsilon_{\mu}(g\mathbf{p})}}B^{g}_{\mu^{\prime}\mu}(\mathbf{p})\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{g\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{k},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{k},\alpha,\downarrow}=\sum_{\mu^{\prime}}\eta^{\dagger}_{g\mathbf{p},\mu^{\prime}}B^{g}_{\mu^{\prime}\mu}(\mathbf{p})\ .

(212)

At the high symmetry points,
B_{g}(\mathbf{p})
is the representation matrix of the bands of
h(\mathbf{p})
, and hence is determined by the irreps of the Cooper pair bands. Hence, the symmetry and topology of the single-particle boson Hamiltonian
h(\mathbf{p})
is detectable through
B_{g}(\mathbf{p})
in the many-body states. We emphasize that although the bound states Cooper pairs are always spin-singlets, they can have nontrivial symmetry representations (
p
-wave,
d
-wave, etc) due to their multi-orbital nature. This is perhaps unfamiliar because
with only spin indices
, a Cooper pair wavefunction which is odd under
\mathbf{p}\to-\mathbf{p}
must be a spin triplet because the spin indices are anti-symmetrized, for example. In contrast, we have shown here that the low-energy Cooper pair bound states, which are spin-singlets, can have be odd under
\mathbf{p}\to-\mathbf{p}
since their orbital indices are anti-symmetrized even though their spin indices are symmetric.

E.5
Topology of the Boson Bands

Let us review the constraints placed on the bands of
h(\mathbf{p})
. First, we chose the electron orbitals to be irreps of a single Wyckoff position in order to guarantee the UPC (App.
A.2
), and we showed that the orbitals of the boson Hamiltonian
h(\mathbf{p})
are at the same Wyckoff position determined by
V[\mathbf{G}]
in Eq. (
206
). (Note that in the bipartite crystalline lattice construction, the Wyckoff position of the boson Hamiltonian is that of the (larger)
L
sublattice.) However, the electron orbitals transform in the representation
D[g]
whereas the boson orbitals transform in the representation
\Gamma[g]
(Eq. (
210
)). Since
\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}[g]=|D_{\alpha\beta}[g]|
, the orbitals of
h(\mathbf{p})
are effectively
s
orbitals.
h(\mathbf{p})
also possesses spinless time-reversal (see Eq. (
205
)). Lastly, we proved that the lowest energy state at the gamma point
\mathbf{p}=0
is always the trivial (
s
) irrep.

We now use Topological Quantum Chemistry
[
26
]
to determine the momentum space irreps which are induced from the boson orbitals satisfying the constraints in the previous paragraph. Because the symmetry of the Cooper pair wavefunction at a given
\mathbf{p}
is determined by the induced momentum space irreps, we can completely classify the possible pairing symmetries and Cooper pair band connectivities in every space group.

First we make the momentum space irreps explicit. Consider a high symmetry momentum
\mathbf{p}
satisfying
g\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{G}
where
\mathbf{G}
is a reciprocal lattice vector. Then
h(\mathbf{p})
commutes with
V^{\dagger}[\mathbf{G}]\Gamma[g]
and the Cooper pair wavefunctions at
\mathbf{p}
transform in an irrep of the little group operators
V^{\dagger}[\mathbf{G}]\Gamma[g]
(see the Appendix of Ref.
[
19
]
). By computing the irreps
V^{\dagger}[\mathbf{G}]\Gamma[g]
for a given model, one can determine the possible pairing symmetries. The Bilbao Crystallographic server has tabulated all such irreps for every Wyckoff position and space group. For instance, in the kagome model in App.
D.4.3
(see Fig.
6
), we computed the irreps at the gamma point to be
\Gamma_{1}
(a
s
-wave 1D irrep) and
\Gamma_{5}
(a 2D
d
-wave irrep, angular momentum
\pm 2
). This is because the little group of the gamma point contains the operator
\Gamma[C_{6}]
which is a
3\times 3
permutation matrix with eigenvalues
1,e^{\pm\frac{2pii}{3}}
. From the Bilbao Crystallographic server, we observe that the
K
point has irreps
K_{1}
(
s
-wave) and
K_{3}
(
d
-wave), and the
M
point has irreps
M_{1}
(
s
-wave) and
M_{3}\oplus M_{4}
(
p
-wave). Note that the
s
-wave irrep at
\Gamma
is at zero energy, so physically this is the mode that condenses, leading to the eta-pairing groundstates over zero momentum and zero angular momentum. However at higher temperature, the higher energy branch is also populated.

Figure 6:
Cooper pair band structure of the Hamiltonian Eq. (
157
) on the kagome sites. (
(a)
) in App.
D.4.3
.
(b)
The band representation computed in App.
D.4.3
is
\mathcal{B}_{1}=\Gamma_{1}+K_{1}+M_{1}
for the low-energy blue band, and
\mathcal{B}_{2}=\Gamma_{5}+K_{3}+M_{3}\oplus M_{4}
for the higher energy pair of red bands.

The global structure of the Cooper pair bands in momentum space is also determined by topological quantum chemistry. In general, a set of bands in momentum space can only be gapped if the irreps obey a set of compatibility relations
[
80
]
. To apply these compatibility relations to the Cooper pair bands, we recall that the Cooper pair orbitals are induced from a single Wyckoff position, the Cooper bands form an elementary band representation (EBR). If the Wyckoff position is non-maximal, then in general the Cooper pair bands will be trivially decomposable: they can be separated into gapped atomic bands. If the Wyckoff position is maximal (like the
3c
kagome position in
G=p6mm
), then there are two possibilities: the EBR could be indecomposable meaning that all Cooper pair bands are connected, or the EBR could be decomposable. If the EBR is decomposable, it can be split into disconnected groups of bands, and necessarily at least one band in this decomposition is topological
[
88
,
87
]
.
The Bilbao crystallographic server tabulates these properties for all EBRs, and explicitly writes the possible irreps of the split EBR. Finally, there is an additional constraint due to time-reversal symmetry which prevents stable topological bands
[
49
]
in one, two, and three dimensions. Thus if a branch of a split EBR has a nonzero symmetry indicator (which would diagnose stable topology if the band were gapped everywhere), there must be a Weyl node in the spectrum which prevents the bands from being gapped everywhere
[
92
,
93
,
26
]
. However, the Cooper pair bands can be gapped with nontrivial topology if the split EBR has a decomposition into fragile topological bands.

Using the Bilbao Crystallographic server, we exhaustively tabulate every EBR in the 230 space groups which is decomposable and obeys the constraints in the first paragraph of App.
E.5
(notably, that there are
s
orbitals on the Wyckoff position). We leave a complete study of the magnetic space groups to other work.

Figure 7:
We show the maximal Wyckoff positions in
p6
and
p6mm
. The 1a position forms a triangular lattice, the 2b position forms a hexagonal lattice, and the 3c position forms a kagome lattice. The site symmetry groups of the positions are
G_{1a}=6,G_{2b}=3,G_{3c}=2
.

To illustrate the method, we first consider the 2D wallpaper groups with spinless time-reversal symmetry. Almost every Wyckoff position is indecomposable, meaning that the bands are all connected through high symmetry points in the BZ. Of the 17 spinless wallpaper groups, only
p61^{\prime}
and
p6mm1^{\prime}
have decomposable bands induced from the trivial irrep at a maximal Wyckoff position. In both cases, this Wyckoff position is the 3c position (see Fig.
7
) which has site symmetry group
G_{3c}=2=\{1,C_{2}\}
. Putting
s
or
p
electron orbitals at the 3c position yields the Cooper pair representations
\Gamma[C_{2}]=\mathbb{1}
and
\Gamma[C_{6}]=P_{3}
where
P_{3}
is the permutation matrix exchanging the 3 sites. This is the representation of an
s
-orbital at the 3c position, denoted
A_{3c}\uparrow G
. From the Bilbao crystallographic server we find that in
G=p61^{\prime}
(the
1^{\prime}
denotes time-reversal), there are two possible band decompositions

\displaystyle A_{3c}\uparrow p61^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{3}\Gamma_{5}+K_{1}\oplus K_{2}K_{3}+M_{1}\oplus M_{2}\oplus M_{2}

(213)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(\Gamma_{1}+K_{1}+M_{1})+(\Gamma_{3}\Gamma_{5}+K_{2}K_{3}+2M_{2})&\\
(\Gamma_{1}+K_{1}+M_{2})+(\Gamma_{3}\Gamma_{5}+K_{2}K_{3}+M_{1}\oplus M_{2})&\\
\end{cases}

where the parentheses denote which bands are connected. Both decompositions contain a single band with a
\Gamma_{1}
irrep (the trivial) irreps, which is the quadratic band at low energy. The two higher bands are connected and gapped from the low energy band. The momentum space irreps are defined

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}61^{\prime}&1&C_{6}\\
\hline\cr\Gamma_{1}&1&1\\
\Gamma_{3}\Gamma_{5}&2&-1\\
\end{array},\qquad\begin{array}[]{r|rr}3&1&C_{3}\\
\hline\cr K_{1}&1&1\\
K_{2}K_{3}&2&-1\\
\end{array},\qquad\begin{array}[]{c|cc}m&1&M_{x}\\
\hline\cr M_{1}&1&1\\
M_{2}&1&-1\\
\end{array}\ .

(214)

We now consider the topology of the two branches in Eq. (
213
). We observe that the
\Gamma_{1}+K_{1}+M_{1}
band is
A_{1a}\uparrow p61^{\prime}
and hence is a trivial atomic band. Its complement
\Gamma_{3}\Gamma_{5}+K_{2}K_{3}+2M_{2}=A_{3c}\uparrow G\ominus A_{1a}\uparrow G
is fragile topological. This is an instance of the general result that gapping band induced from a maximal Wyckoff position must result in topology
[
88
,
87
]
: since the lower band is trivial, the higher two bands must be topological. The other allowed decomposition has a
\Gamma_{1}+K_{1}+M_{2}
band. Using the Smith normal form
[
82
]
, we compute the symmetry indicator
\theta=m(\Gamma_{2})+m(M_{2})\mod 2
, which is the number of odd inversion eigenvalues mod 2. We observe that
\theta=1
for
\Gamma_{1}+K_{1}+M_{2}
, indicating is must have Weyl nodes connecting it to the other bands.

We systematize this information in the following table. W.P. stands for the Wyckoff position of the orbitals. The
\eta
irreps column denotes the orbital of
h(\mathbf{p})
of the site symmetry group of the Wyckoff position (physically it is always an
s
orbital, but is denoted with different symbols in different space groups), and
e^{-}
irrep denotes the possible electron orbitals that yield the bosonic orbital through
\Gamma[g]=|D_{\alpha\beta}[g]|
.
\#
numbers the possible decompositions and
\theta
is the symmetry indicator, which results in Weyl nodes due to
\Gamma[\mathcal{T}]
. Our classification enumerates the topology of the Cooper pair bands (the first entry corresponds to the gapless quadratic band). When the band is trivial, its atomic representation is given. Otherwise fragile topology or topologically protected Weyl nodes are indicated.

We see that in 2D, the only cases with decomposable bands at maximal Wyckoff positions are in
p6
and
p6mm
. In all other groups and maximal Wyckoff positions, the Cooper pair bands are connected. In the decomposable cases listed above, only the higher energy bands can be fragile topological.

\Gamma
point. In the case where the symmetry indicator is zero, we check whether the band is fragile or atomic. They are enumerated in the following table. There are many cases where the low-lying Cooper pair band has topologically protected Weyl nodes. We also highlight
G=Fd\bar{3}c
, space group 228, where the only allowed decomposition is into fragile bands.
