Appendix F
Ten Band Model for Twisted Bilayer Graphene

In this section, we put forward a variation of the ten band model discussed in Ref.
37
. Our ten band model captures the flat band irreps of twisted bilayer graphene via an
S
-matrix construction consisting of
6
orbitals in the
L
sublattice and
4
orbitals in the
\tilde{L}
sublattice, yielding two flat bands. However, we compute the Wilson loop
[
36
,
115
]
of the flat bands in our model and find it has winding number
2
, instead of winding number
1
as in the Bistritzer-MacDonald Hamiltonian
[
36
,
37
,
39
]
. In contrast, the model of Ref.
[
37
]
, however, in the larger lattice has orbitals at both the
1a
and
3f
positions, and thus does not satisfy uniform pairing because the orbitals at the different Wyckoff positions are not related by symmetry.

To remedy this issue we propose a modified
S
-matrix which instead has in the
L
lattice
s
orbitals in the 6j position, which are related by
C_{6z}\mathcal{T}
and hence have uniform pairing. The
\tilde{L}
lattice possesses
p_{x},p_{y}
orbitals at the
2c
positions (the hexagon). In SSG
177.151
, the irreps induced by the two sublattices read

\displaystyle E_{2c}\uparrow G

\displaystyle=2\Gamma_{3}+(2K_{1}\oplus K_{2}K_{3})+(2M_{1}\oplus 2M_{2})

(215)

\displaystyle A_{6j}\uparrow G

\displaystyle=(\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}\oplus 2\Gamma_{3})+(2K_{1}\oplus 2K_{2}K_{3})+(3M_{1}\oplus 3M_{2})

(216)

\displaystyle{\cal B}_{\text{FB}}

\displaystyle=(\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2})+K_{2}K_{3}+(M_{1}\oplus M_{2}).

(217)

The irreps of the flat band correctly match the irreps carried by the flat bands in twisted bilayer graphene, and thus these two bands must be topological. Note that as this is a lattice model, one cannot correctly implement the particle-hole symmetry that protects the strong topology in TBG
[
116
]
. The real-space Hamiltonian involves only NN hopping and NNN hopping between
s
and
p
orbitals, and there are only four complex independent parameters:

\displaystyle H_{\text{kin}}^{\nu,\sigma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bf{R}}}t_{NN,x}^{\nu,\sigma}c^{\dagger}_{{\bf{R}},1,\sigma,\nu}d_{{\bf{R}},p_{x},1,\sigma,\nu}+t_{NN,y}^{\nu,\sigma}c^{\dagger}_{{\bf{R}},1,\sigma,\nu}d_{{\bf{R}},p_{y},1,\sigma,\nu}

\displaystyle+t_{NNN,x}^{\nu,\sigma}c^{\dagger}_{{\bf{R}},1,\sigma,\nu}d_{{\bf{R}}-{\bf{a}}_{2},p_{x},2,\sigma,\nu}+t_{NNN,y}^{\nu,\sigma}c^{\dagger}_{{\bf{R}},1,\sigma,\nu}d_{{\bf{R}}-{\bf{a}}_{2},p_{y},2,\sigma,\nu}+(p6^{\prime})+(H.c.)

(218)

where
\sigma
denotes the spin and
\nu
denotes the valley, giving four identical copies of the kinetic Hamiltonian. Here the
c
orbitals belong to the
L
sublattice, and are labeled
\alpha=1\dots 6
, labeling each of the
6j
positions, while the
d
orbitals belong to the
\tilde{L}
sublattice and are labeled
\beta=\{p_{x},1\},\{p_{y},1\},\{p_{x},2\},\{p_{y},2\}
, labeling the
2c
positions. In addition to the spin degree of freedom, the TBG model has a valley degree of freedom,
\nu
, from the modes arising from the Dirac cones from the
K
and
K^{\prime}
points in the BZ.
p6^{\prime}
indicates that the appropriate symmetry-related terms are added to yield the full tight-binding Hamiltonians. We find that the choice of parameters

\displaystyle t_{NN,x}^{\nu=+1,\sigma=\uparrow}

\displaystyle=76.50+2.69i,t_{NN,y}^{\nu=+1,\sigma=\uparrow}=-14.64+35.81i,

\displaystyle t_{NNN,x}^{\nu=+1,\sigma=\uparrow}

\displaystyle=12.17-7.44i,t_{NNN,y}^{\nu=+1,\sigma=\uparrow}=5.47-5.45i

(219)

yields an excellent fit of the low-lying bands to the BM model, as depicted in Fig.
8
(b). The lattice vectors are
{\bf{a}}_{1}=(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2},-\frac{1}{2})a_{0},{\bf{a}}_{2}=(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2},-\frac{1}{2})a_{0}
, where
a_{0}
is the unit cell length.

Figure 8:
We summarize the 10-band UPC model of TBG.
(a)
shows the electron orbitals of the model in the unit cell,
(b)
shows the single-particle bands with the Wilson loop over the flat bands inset,
(c)
shows the Cooper pair spectrum, and
(d)
shows the mean-field superfluid weight at finite temperature.

The main difference between this ten-band model and previous models arises from the addition of the valley degree of freedom. The most natural Hubbard-type interaction would be one that includes all spin and valleys summed together in the density:

\displaystyle H=\dfrac{|U|}{2}\sum_{{\bf{R}},\alpha}\left(\sum_{\nu,\sigma}{\bar{n}}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\sigma,\nu}\right)^{2}.

(220)

However, we find that this interaction is not tractable with our eta-pairing formalism. Instead, however, the slightly different interaction

\displaystyle H=\frac{|U|}{2}\sum_{{\bf{R}},\alpha}({\bar{S}}^{z}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\nu\sigma=+1})^{2}+({\bar{S}}^{z}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\nu\sigma=-1})^{2},

(221)

where the spin-valley operators
{\bar{S}}^{z}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\nu\sigma}
are defined as

\displaystyle{\bar{S}}^{z}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\nu\sigma=+1}

\displaystyle={\bar{n}}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\nu=+1,\sigma=\uparrow}-{\bar{n}}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\nu=-1,\sigma=\downarrow}

(222)

\displaystyle{\bar{S}}^{z}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\nu\sigma=-1}

\displaystyle={\bar{n}}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\nu=+1,\sigma=\downarrow}-{\bar{n}}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\nu=-1,\sigma=\uparrow},

(223)

can be recast into an eta-pairing problem. So long as
U(1)
valley,
U(1)
spin, and
\cal T
are preserved, there are two eta-pairing operators that commute with the interaction Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\eta_{+1}^{\dagger}=\sum_{{\bf{k}},m}\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}},m,\nu=+1,\sigma=\uparrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-{\bf{k}},m,\nu=-1,\sigma=\downarrow}

(224)

\displaystyle\eta_{-1}^{\dagger}=\sum_{{\bf{k}},m}\gamma^{\dagger}_{{\bf{k}},m,\nu=+1,\sigma=\downarrow}\gamma^{\dagger}_{-{\bf{k}},m,\nu=-1,\sigma=\uparrow}.

(225)

This spin-valley-polarized model splits into two eta-pairing models, each giving the Cooper pair spectrum shown in Fig.
1
(c). The two Hamiltonians are labeled by spin-valley,
\nu\sigma=+1
and
\nu\sigma=-1
, and are

\displaystyle H^{\nu\sigma=+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\nu=+1}H^{\nu,\sigma}_{\text{kin}}+\frac{|U|}{2}\sum_{{\bf{R}},\alpha}({\bar{S}}^{z}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\nu\sigma=+1})^{2}

(226)

\displaystyle H^{\nu\sigma=-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\nu=-1}H^{\nu,\sigma}_{\text{kin}}+\frac{|U|}{2}\sum_{{\bf{R}},\alpha}({\bar{S}}^{z}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\nu\sigma=-1})^{2}.

(227)

We now deduce the irreducible representations of the Cooper pair bands. Let us focus on the
\nu\sigma=+1
sector. As argued and rigorously proved in App.
E
, the Cooper pairs themselves transform like
s
orbitals induced from the larger sublattice
L
, which in this case is the 6j position. We offer here an intuitive argument for why this must be so. Consider an
unprojected
Cooper pair operator at site
{\bf{R}}
and flavor
\alpha
, in the larger sublattice
L
:

\displaystyle\eta_{{\bf{R}},\alpha}^{\dagger}=c^{\dagger}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\uparrow}c^{\dagger}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\downarrow}.

(228)

Let us assume there is one orbital per position (at least in the larger sublattice
L
), so that the crystalline symmetry
g
is a permutation matrix up to a phase: the orbital
\alpha
is located at position
{\bf x}_{\alpha}
, and under
g
this is mapped to orbital
\beta
located at position
{\bf x}_{\beta}=g{\bf x}_{\alpha}
. Under a crystalline symmetry
g
, this operator transforms as

\displaystyle g\eta_{{\bf{R}},\alpha}^{\dagger}g^{-1}

\displaystyle=gc^{\dagger}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\uparrow}c^{\dagger}_{{\bf{R}},\alpha,\downarrow}g^{-1}

(229)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}}D[g]_{\alpha\beta}D^{*}[g]_{\alpha\beta^{\prime}}c^{\dagger}_{g{\bf{R}},\beta,\uparrow}c^{\dagger}_{g{\bf{R}},\beta^{\prime},\downarrow}

(230)

\displaystyle=\eta_{g{\bf{R}},\beta}^{\dagger},~~{\bf x}_{\beta}=g{\bf x}_{\alpha}.

(231)

Any phases introduced in the permutation matrix
D
is canceled by the complex conjugate from the time-reversed
D
arising from the opposite spin. Thus, these
\eta
operators transform as
s
orbitals. When projecting into the flat bands, the orbitals belonging to the smaller sublattice
\tilde{L}
possess no wavefunction weight, and so those bands do not participate in the Cooper pair Hamiltonian, leaving
s
orbitals in the larger
L
sublattice.

In our TBG model, these irreps read

\displaystyle A_{6j}\uparrow G

\displaystyle=(\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}\oplus 2\Gamma_{3})+(2K_{1}\oplus 2K_{2}K_{3})+(3M_{1}\oplus 3M_{2}).

(232)

The
6j
position is non-maximal, so the bands form a composite representation which can in principle be separated into gapped bands. The
\Gamma_{1}
irrep is the zero-energy Cooper pair which has
s
-wave symmetry. The
\Gamma_{2}
irrep is odd under
C_{2x}
and is of
p_{z}
orbital character. The
\Gamma_{3}
irrep is formed from angular momentum
\pm 1
states under the
C_{3z}
operator, and so is of
p_{x},p_{y}
orbital character (see Table
F
below).

1

C_{3z}

C_{2x}

\Gamma_{1}

1

1

1

\Gamma_{2}

1

1

-1

\Gamma_{3}

2

-1

0

Table 4:
Irreps of the group
32
which is the unitary subgroup of
6^{\prime}22^{\prime}
, the little group of the Gamma point in twisted bilayer graphene.

Appendix G
Mean-field superfluid weight in degenerate flat bands

In this section, we derive the mean-field superfluid weight of a set of
N_{f}
degenerate isolated flat bands with uniform pairing. We take
e,\hbar,k_{B}=1
. In this section only, we use bra-ket notation for the single-particle eigenstates since we are working at the mean-field level. Our calculation is straightforward generalization of the
N_{f}=1
case demonstrated in Ref.
[
61
]
. The result agrees with prior work (Ref.
[
17
]
) on degenerate flat bands, but correctly accounts for the minimal quantum metric in the self-consistent mean-field solution
[
61
]
.

We describe our system with the attractive mean-field approximated Hubbard model

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\boldsymbol{c}_{\mathbf{k}}^{{\dagger}}H_{\rm BdG}(\mathbf{k})\boldsymbol{c}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\vphantom{{\dagger}}}+\sum_{\mathbf{k}}{\rm Tr}\,\tilde{h}^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{k})-N_{orb}\mathcal{N}\mu-\mathcal{N}\sum_{\alpha}\frac{|\Delta_{\alpha}|^{2}}{U},

(233)

\displaystyle H_{\rm BdG}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{h}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{k})-\mu\mathbb{1}_{N_{orb}}&\boldsymbol{\Delta}\\
\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{{\dagger}}&-\tilde{h}^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{k})+\mu\mathbb{1}_{N_{orb}}\end{pmatrix},

(234)

where
\mu
is the chemical potential,
\tilde{h}^{\sigma}
is the
single-particle hopping matrix for spin
\sigma
and

\boldsymbol{c}_{\mathbf{k}}^{\vphantom{{\dagger}}}=(c_{\mathbf{k},\alpha=1,\uparrow}^{\vphantom{{\dagger}}},\ldots,c_{\mathbf{k},\alpha=N_{orb},\uparrow}^{\vphantom{{\dagger}}},c_{-\mathbf{k},\alpha=1,\downarrow}^{{\dagger}},\ldots,c_{-\mathbf{k},\alpha=N_{orb},\downarrow}^{{\dagger}})
. The
matrix
\boldsymbol{\Delta}={\rm diag}(\Delta_{1},\ldots,\Delta_{N_{orb}})

contains the mean-field order parameters
\Delta_{\alpha}=\Delta_{i\alpha}=U\langle c_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\downarrow}^{\vphantom{{\dagger}}}c_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\uparrow}^{\vphantom{{\dagger}}}\rangle
, where

c_{\mathbf{R}\alpha\sigma}^{\vphantom{{\dagger}}}
destroys a particle with spin
\sigma
at
orbital
\alpha
in the unit cell
\mathbf{R}
. We assume the order parameters
are independent of the unit cell. Since the we have shown in App.
D
that the lowest energy gap is
s
-wave, zero momentum, and has the wavefunction
\propto\sum_{\mathbf{R},\alpha}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\uparrow}\bar{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},\alpha,\downarrow}
, we expect our mean-field ansatz to accurately capture this Cooper pair branch.

The superfluid weight for this Hamiltonian is given
by
[
61
]
(where
\partial_{i}=\partial/\partial k_{i}
)

\displaystyle[D_{s}]_{ij}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}V_{c}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},ab}\frac{n_{F}(E_{a})-n_{F}(E_{b})}{E_{b}-E_{a}}\big[

\displaystyle\bra{\psi_{a}}\partial_{i}\widetilde{H}_{\mathbf{k}}\ket{\psi_{b}}\bra{\psi_{b}}\partial_{j}\widetilde{H}_{\mathbf{k}}\ket{\psi_{a}}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\bra{\psi_{a}}(\partial_{i}\widetilde{H}_{\mathbf{k}}\gamma^{z}+\delta_{i}\Delta)\ket{\psi_{b}}\bra{\psi_{b}}(\partial_{j}\widetilde{H}_{\mathbf{k}}\gamma^{z}+\delta_{j}\Delta)\ket{\psi_{a}}\big]-\frac{1}{V_{c}}C_{ij},

(235)

and

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\widetilde{H_{\mathbf{k}}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial\tilde{h}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})}{\partial k_{i}^{\prime}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}=\mathbf{k}}&0\\
0&\frac{\partial\tilde{h}^{\downarrow}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})^{*}}{\partial k_{i}^{\prime}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}=-\mathbf{k}}\end{pmatrix},\ \delta_{i}\Delta=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{{\rm d}\boldsymbol{\Delta}}{{\rm d}q_{i}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{q}=\boldsymbol{0}}\\
\frac{{\rm d}\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{{\dagger}}}{{\rm d}q_{i}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{q}=\boldsymbol{0}}&0\end{pmatrix},\ C_{ij}=\frac{1}{U}\sum_{\alpha}\frac{{\rm d}\Delta_{\alpha}}{{\rm d}q_{i}}\frac{{\rm d}\Delta_{\alpha}^{*}}{{\rm d}q_{j}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{q}=\boldsymbol{0}}+{\rm H.c.}

(236)

The vector
\mathbf{q}
in the derivatives
{\rm d}\Delta_{\alpha}/{\rm d}q_{i}
corresponds to the insertion of an electromagnetic field with
\mathbf{A}\sim\mathbf{q}
, which changes the self-consistent order parameters as
\Delta_{i\alpha}\to\Delta_{i\alpha}e^{2i\mathbf{q}\cdot(\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{r}_{\alpha})}
. The Fermi distribution is
n_{F}(E)=1/(1+e^{\beta E})
, where
\beta=1/T
is the inverse temperature. The eigenvalues
and eigenvectors of
H_{\rm BdG}
are
E_{a}
and
\ket{\psi_{a}}

respectively, and
\gamma_{z}=\sigma_{z}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{N_{orb}}
,
where
\sigma_{i}
are the Pauli matrices. The coefficient

[n_{F}(E_{a})-n_{F}(E_{b})]/(E_{b}-E_{a})
should be understood as
-\partial n_{F}(E)/\partial E
when
E_{a}=E_{b}
. The total volume of the system is

\mathcal{N}V_{c}
where
V_{c}
is the volume of a unit cell.

We will focus on the superfluid weight for a set of
degenerate flat bands
\mathcal{B}
with energy

\epsilon_{\overline{m}}
(
\bar{m}
denotes a flat band) in a system with time-reversal symmetry
(
\tilde{h}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{k})=[\tilde{h}^{\downarrow}(-\mathbf{k})]^{*}
)
and uniform pairing. Due to time-reversal symmetry, there always
exists a choice of intra-unit-cell orbital positions which guarantees
that the derivatives of the order parameters at
\mathbf{q}=\boldsymbol{0}

vanish
[
61
]
. With the uniform pairing condition, these positions are given by Eq. (
200
). Let us assume that this is the
chosen set of positions, so that the superfluid weight of
Eq.
235
simplifies to

[D_{s}]_{ij}=\frac{1}{V_{c}\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},ab}\frac{n_{F}(E_{a})-n_{F}(E_{b})}{E_{b}-E_{a}}\big[\bra{\psi_{a}}\partial_{i}\widetilde{H}_{\mathbf{k}}\ket{\psi_{b}}\bra{\psi_{b}}\partial_{j}\widetilde{H}_{\mathbf{k}}\ket{\psi_{a}}-\bra{\psi_{a}}\partial_{i}\widetilde{H}_{\mathbf{k}}\gamma^{z}\ket{\psi_{b}}\bra{\psi_{b}}\partial_{j}\widetilde{H}_{\mathbf{k}}\gamma^{z}\ket{\psi_{a}}\big],

(237)

This can be expressed in terms of the dispersion relations and Bloch
functions of the bands by expressing the eigenvectors of
H_{\rm BdG}

as
\ket{\psi_{a}}=\sum_{m=1}^{N_{orb}}(w_{+,am}\ket{+}\otimes\ket{m_{\mathbf{k}}}+w_{-,am}\ket{-}\otimes\ket{m_{\mathbf{k}}})
,
where
\ket{m_{\mathbf{k}}}
is the eigenvector of

\tilde{h}^{\uparrow}(\mathbf{k})
with eigenvalue
\epsilon_{m,\mathbf{k}}
. We
denote the eigenvectors of
\sigma_{z}
with eigenvalues
\pm 1
by

\ket{\pm}
. With these definitions, Eq. (
237
) can
be rewritten as

\displaystyle[D_{s}]_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{V_{c}\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{mn}C_{pq}^{mn}[j_{i}(\mathbf{k})]_{mn}[j_{j}(\mathbf{k})]_{pq},

(238)

\displaystyle C_{pq}^{mn}

\displaystyle=4\sum_{ab}\frac{n_{F}(E_{a})-n_{F}(E_{b})}{E_{b}-E_{a}}w_{+,am}^{*}w_{+,bn}w_{-,bp}^{*}w_{-,aq},

(239)

\displaystyle[j_{i}]_{mn}

\displaystyle=\bra{m_{\sigma,\mathbf{k}}}\partial_{i}\tilde{h}^{\sigma}(\mathbf{k})\ket{n_{\sigma,\mathbf{k}}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{mn}\partial_{i}\epsilon_{m,\mathbf{k}}+(\epsilon_{m,\mathbf{k}}-\epsilon_{n,\mathbf{k}})\langle\partial_{i}m_{\mathbf{k}}|n_{\mathbf{k}}\rangle.

(240)

In a system with uniform pairing, we can make the ansatz

\boldsymbol{\Delta}=\Delta\mathbb{1}
,
and
H_{\rm BdG}=\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{orb}}[(\epsilon_{m}-\mu)^{2}\sigma^{z}+\Delta^{2}\sigma^{x}]\otimes\ket{m}\bra{m}
, where the momentum dependence of
\epsilon_{m}(\mathbf{k})
and
\ket{m_{\mathbf{k}}}
is suppressed. The
eigenvalues of
H_{BdG}
are then
\pm E_{m}=\pm\sqrt{(\epsilon_{m}-\mu)^{2}+\Delta^{2}}
, and the corresponding
eigenfunctions are
\ket{\psi_{m}^{+}}=(u_{m}\ket{+}+v_{m}\ket{-})\otimes\ket{m}
and
\ket{\psi_{m}^{-}}=(-v_{m}\ket{+}+u_{m}\ket{-})\otimes\ket{m}
, where (again suppressing the momentum dependence)

u_{m}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{1+\frac{\epsilon_{m}-\mu}{E_{m}}},\>\>v_{m}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{1-\frac{\epsilon_{m}-\mu}{E_{n}}}.

(241)

As Eq.
240
shows, all contributions to the superfluid weight which involve

[j_{i}^{\sigma}]_{mn}
with
m,n\in\mathcal{B}
vanish (because
\epsilon_{m}(\mathbf{k})
=
\epsilon_{n}(\mathbf{k})
=
\epsilon_{\overline{m}}
for the degenerate flat bands) vanish. Thus the
superfluid weight reduces to the geometric contribution

\displaystyle[D_{s}]_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{2|\Delta|^{2}}{V_{c}\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{B}}\sum_{n\notin\mathcal{B}}\left[\frac{{\rm tanh}(\beta E_{\overline{m}}/2)}{E_{\overline{m}}}-\frac{{\rm tanh}(\beta E_{n}/2)}{E_{n}}\right]\frac{\epsilon_{n}-\epsilon_{\overline{m}}}{\epsilon_{n}+\epsilon_{\overline{m}}-2\mu}\left(\langle\partial_{i}m|n\rangle\langle n|\partial_{j}m\rangle+{\rm H.c.}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{|\Delta|^{2}}{V_{c}\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\neq n\\
m,n\notin\mathcal{B}\end{subarray}}\left[\frac{{\rm tanh}(\beta E_{m}/2)}{E_{m}}-\frac{{\rm tanh}(\beta E_{n}/2)}{E_{n}}\right]\frac{\epsilon_{n}-\epsilon_{m}}{\epsilon_{n}+\epsilon_{m}-2\mu}\left(\langle\partial_{i}m|n\rangle\langle n|\partial_{j}m\rangle+{\rm H.c.}\right).

(242)

Let
W_{n}
be the band gap between the bands in
\mathcal{B}
and the

n
th band. We now assume that
W_{n}\gg|\epsilon_{\overline{m}}-\mu|

and
W_{n}\gg|\Delta|
for all
n\notin\mathcal{B}
, and the superfluid
weight reduces to
[
117
]

[D_{s}]_{ij}=\frac{4\Delta^{2}}{V_{c}}\frac{{\rm tanh}(\beta E_{\overline{m}}/2)}{E_{\overline{m}}}g_{ij},

(243)

where
g_{ij}=(1/\mathcal{N})\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{B}}\sum_{n\notin\mathcal{B}}{\rm Re}\langle\partial_{i}m|n\rangle\langle n|\partial_{j}m\rangle
is the
integral of the quantum metric of the set of bands
\mathcal{B}
. Since we assumed
above that the derivatives of the order parameters are zero, this
is in fact the minimal quantum metric
[
61
]
.

At
T=0
, the filling and the pairing gap are given by

\displaystyle N_{f}\mathcal{N}\nu

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k}}v^{2}{\rm Tr}\,P_{\mathbf{k}}\,=N_{f}\mathcal{N}v^{2}

(244)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha}=\Delta

\displaystyle=\frac{|U|}{\mathcal{N}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}}uv[P_{\mathbf{k}}]_{\alpha\alpha}=\epsilon|U|\sqrt{\nu(1-\nu)},

(245)

with
\epsilon=N_{f}/N_{L}
, where
N_{L}
is the number of orbitals where the flat band
states have a nonzero weight. We have used that
v=v_{\bar{m}},u=u_{\bar{m}}
are independent of
\mathbf{k}
and
\bar{m}
for a flat band (see Eq. Eq. (
241
)) and that

{\rm Tr}\,P=N_{f}
. Noticing that

|\Delta|/E_{\overline{m}}=2uv=2\sqrt{\nu(1-\nu)}
,
we get the zero-temperature superfluid weight

[D_{s}]_{ij}=8\frac{|U|\epsilon}{V_{c}}\nu(1-\nu)g_{ij}.

(246)
